SECTION 2: Big O
########################################

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.1: Big O: Intro
----------------------------------------

In this course, we're going to spend a lot of time on big O. It is a huge topic when it comes to data structures and algorithms. So what is big O? Big O is a way of comparing two sets of code. And let's say code one and code two accomplish exactly the same thing. How would you compare one against the other? Code one might be more readable. That might be better. Koto might be more concise, the code might take up fewer lines, and if that's what you're measuring, then you would say that code two is better.

Big O is a way of comparing code one and code two mathematically about how efficient they run. In a coding interview, you will absolutely be asked questions about big O. So let's look at what big O is. Let's say we have a stopwatch and we run code one, and we start the stopwatch, and it runs for 15 seconds, and we reset the stopwatch, and we run code two, and code two runs a lot longer than 15 seconds. It runs for a full minute. Based on this, you would say that code one is better than code two.

You can measure it. This is called time complexity. The thing about time complexity that is interesting is that it is not measured in time. Because if you took the same code and ran it on a computer that runs twice as fast, it would complete twice as fast. It doesn't make the code any better. It just means the computer is better. So it is measured in the number of operations that it takes to complete something. And we'll look at examples of that as we go along here.

In addition to time complexity, we measure space complexity. So let's say that code one. While it runs very fast comparatively, let's say it takes up a lot of memory when it runs. And maybe code two, even though that takes much longer to finish. Maybe it takes up less memory. If preserving memory space is your most important priority and you don't mind having some extra time complexity, maybe code two is better. So when you get into an interview, there's a good chance that you'll be asked questions about time complexity.

And then they'll say, hey, well, what if space complexity is our main priority? How would you approach it then? You have to understand both concepts and be able to address that in the job interview. But for the most part in this class we will be dealing. With time complexity. And that is our quick intro to big O.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.2: Big O: Worst Case
----------------------------------------

So when dealing with time and space complexity, you will see these three Greek letters. They are omega, theta and Omicron. Omicron is better known as O as in big O. And to show how we use these, I'm going to bring up an array and say we're going to use a for loop to loop through this array. So when we're looking at how many times that we have to run the for loop, our best case would be if we're looking for the number one, our worst case would be if we were looking for the number seven.

And our average case would be if we were looking for the number four. So this best case with the number one for that we use omega. For the average case we use the Greek letter theta. And for our worst case we use the Greek letter omicron or O. So one of the things people will say when you talk about your big O being a particular time complexity is they'll say things like, yeah, but what is your average big O or your best case, big O? Well, technically there is no average case or best case.

Big O average case would be theta and best case would be omega. But you will hear people use it in that way. But technically big O is always worst case.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.3: Big O: O(n)
----------------------------------------

So now let's look at our first big O notation. It is O of n and I start with o of n, not because it is the most efficient or the least efficient. I start with this because I think it's the easiest one to get your head around. So to explain this, I'm going to use a simple method. We're going to call it print items. We will pass it an integer n. And then we'll have a for loop that is going to run n times. And then we will print out I from the for loop.

So now let's go take a look at this in IntelliJ. So there is that method there. And then what we're going to do down here is we're going to run that method with the number ten. And I'll run this. And you can see over here that it printed out zero through nine. So it printed out ten items. So now let's flip back. So this code here is a within. And the reason that it is O of N is we had a number n. In this case it was ten. And then we did ten operations and our for loop.

Now let's look at O of n on a graph. So on this graph, on this axis, this is in and this is the number of operations. O of n is always going to be a straight line. It is what is called proportional. So as we move through the big O section we will add different big O's to this graph. So we can compare one to another. But for now that is o of n.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.4: Big O: Drop Constants
----------------------------------------

So in big O, we have a few ways that we can simplify things. And the first of those that we're going to look at is drop constants. So let's bring up the method that we had in the last video that looked like this with a single for loop. And with this we'll add a second for loop. And both for loops are going to run n times. And so to run this let's flip over to IntelliJ. So there is our new print items method there. And once again down here we're going to run this with the number ten.

And I'll run this now. And you can see here that this printed out zero through nine here. And then it printed out zero through nine again here. Okay. So let's flip back. So in this case we had n plus n operations. We passed this ten items and it ran 20 times which is two n. So you might think that this would be O of two n. But we simplify this by dropping the constant and it just becomes O of n. And it doesn't matter if the constant is 2 or 10 or a thousand.

We're going to remove it and just leave this with o of n. So our first rule of simplification. Is dropped. Constants.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.5: Big O: O(n^2)
----------------------------------------

Now let's look at o of n squared. So I'm going to bring up our code from our last video here. This was a for loop followed by another for loop. To make this code O of n squared, all we have to do is make it where one for loop is inside of the other for loop. Like this. Now let's flip over to IntelliJ and take a look at this. So there is our method there with one for loop inside of another for loop. And I want to point out with this line of code here that it's important to have these quotes because it turns the whole thing into a string.

If you don't, it's going to take I and J and add them together as integers, and you're not going to get the output that you expect. So I'm going to run this. And then over here I'm going to scroll up to the top. Here we get zero zero and it goes all the way down at the bottom. Here it goes to nine nine. So now let's flip back. So these four loops ran n times n times or n squared. So we pass this a ten. And we had 100 lines that were output. And that is O of n squared.

So now let's take a look at this on the graph. So you can see compared to O of n, that O of n squared grows much faster. And that means from a time complexity perspective it is less efficient. So if you have a situation where your code is O of n squared, and there's a way of rewriting the code to make it O of n, that is a huge gain in efficiency. So that. Is O of n squared.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.6: Big O: Drop Non-Dominants
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to look at another way that we can simplify big O. That's called drop non dominance. So to illustrate this I'm going to start with our nested for loops that we saw in the last video. But I'm going to add an additional for loop after these. So we have our nested for loops above and a separate standalone for loop down here. So let's flip over and take a look at this in IntelliJ. So there is our method there. And we'll call this down here with the number ten.

And I'll run this. Okay. So I'm going to scroll to the top here. And you can see it starts with zero zero and then goes down to here where it goes to nine nine. And then we have zero through nine. So now let's flip back. So this nested for loop ran o of n squared times. That was the zero 0 to 9 nine. And this for loop ran o of n times. So the total number of operations was n squared plus n. So that would be o of n squared plus n. But as n gets larger the n squared grows much faster than the n.

Even if we only took this to 100, the n squared portion would be 10,000 operations and that standalone n would be 100 operations. So the n squared is the dominant firm and the n is the non-dominant firm. And as n becomes larger and larger, you can see that that standalone n starts becoming irrelevant. So we just drop the n. So this rule of simplification. Is dropped. Non dominance.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.7: Big O: O(1)
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to take a look at O of one. So I'm going to bring up a method that we're going to call add items. We'll pass it an integer n. And all we're going to do is return n plus n. So it doesn't matter if n is ten or n is a billion, there's only going to be one operation, which is the addition, and that is O of one. With what we've seen before. As in grows the number of operations grows in this situation. As n grows, the number of operations will stay the same.

So now let's change this from being n plus n to being n plus n plus n. Now we have two operations. So we could call this O of two. And as you may have guessed, we are going to simplify this and call it O of one. O of one is also called constant time. It doesn't necessarily mean that there's only going to be one operation. It means that as n grows, the number of operations stays constant. So now let's take a look at this on the graph. Over one is just going to be a flat line across the bottom.

It is the most efficient big O. If you can do something in O of one time, it is always going to be much more efficient than anything else. And that. Izzo of one.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.8: Big O: O(log n)
----------------------------------------

Now let's look at O of log N. And to explain this I'm going to bring up a sorted array. And to do what we're about to do here the data does have to be sorted. So let's say we want to find a particular value in this array. We'll say we're going to look for the number one. What is the quickest way to narrow this down to get to that one. Well the quickest way would be to cut the array in half and see if the one is in the first half or the second half.

So it is not in the second half. So we don't have to look at any of those numbers so we can remove those. And that may not seem like a big deal when you have an array with only eight items in it. But if you had an array with a million items in it, that's a half a million items that you don't have to look at. So that is a pretty big deal. So then we can take the array and do this again. And we know it's not on the second half. So we can remove those.

And then we'll do it again. And then we'll find the number that we're looking for. So let's bring back all of the other items in the array. And look at how many steps this took to find the number one. It was one two. Three steps. And remember we had eight items in this array. And it just so happens that two to the third power is eight. So now I'm going to take this equation and turn it into a logarithm. And we're not going to get really deep into this math.

This will be pretty simple. But when we convert this to a logarithm it looks like this log sub two of eight equals three. And I'm going to put this back and let's look at this again log sub two of eight equals three. So what this is basically saying is two to the watt power equals eight. Two to the what? Power equals eight. Well, two to the third power equals eight. To put it another way, if you took the number eight and you repeatedly divided it by two, how many times would it take to get down to one item.

And that is three times. But the real power of this is when we're dealing with very large numbers. Let's say we take log sub two of this number and this is over a billion. How many times would you need to cut this number in half to get down to one item. And it is 31 times. So you could have an array with over a billion items in it, and find any number in that array in 31 steps. And that is the power of O of log n. So now let's take this and look at it on a graph.

So you can see that this is very flat, very efficient. It's not as efficient as O of one, of course, but it is the next best thing. And so everything we're going to look at this course is going to be one of these four. Except for I'm going to show one other one here. And this one is O of n times log n that is used with some sorting algorithms. So we're going to see this with quicksort and merge sort. But other than that everything else will be one of these four.

So that. Is O of log n.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.9: Big O: Different Terms for Inputs
----------------------------------------

So our next rule for big O is different terms for inputs. So what I'm going to do is walk through some code that we've seen before, and then we're going to make some changes to it. So the code that we saw before is when we have a for loop, and then we follow it with another for loop. And then we say each of the for loops runs in times. And we would say that's O of two n. Except we're going to drop the constant and it becomes o of n. So the change we're going to make to this is instead of passing this one argument, we're going to pass it two arguments.

And then we're going to say the first for loop is going to run a times. And the second for loop is going to run B times. And this is a very popular gotcha interview question. And the reason is because you see a for loop and you say okay that's O of n. Here is another for loop that is also O of n. And then we'll add them together and that becomes O of two n. And we drop the constant. And this is O of N, but that is not correct. If you think about this from an algebraic standpoint, you cannot say that A is equal to n, and b is also equal to n.

You would have to say that this is O of A, and this is O of B, and when you add them together, this becomes o of a plus b. And that is as far as you can simplify this. And the reason for that is if A was one and B was a billion, these would be two very different for loops. And you'd have to understand how each one works to understand the time complexity of the entire method. Likewise, if we took these two for loops and instead of having them one after the other, we had them one inside of the other.

This would become o of a. Times be. So we can't just say that each of the terms is in. We have to use different terms for inputs.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.10: Big O: Array Lists
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to look at the big O of array lists. And I use an array list here instead of arrays. Because to show all of these concepts, I need a data structure that's going to be dynamic and allow us to add new items and not just be of fixed length, but a lot of the concepts that I'm going to show here will apply to arrays as well. Also, instead of showing our array list like this, I'm going to represent it like this so I can show the indexes.

Now we'll just walk through some of the methods for Arraylists and what the big O of those methods are. So we'll start out with my list for my array list. And say dot, add with the number 17 that's going to do this. When we add something to this end of the array list. It's a pretty simple process because we don't have any reindexing like all the way down. These indexes do not need to be changed. Likewise, when we remove this item with a dot, remove at the index of four and we remove that, there is nothing that needs to be re-indexed and the array list.

So it is one operation to add something. It is one operation to remove something. So this is O of one. This is different. If we do something on the other end of the array list. So if we say my list dot, remove the item at the index of zero. This one right here. When we remove this the problem that we have. Is this is no longer at the correct index. So we need to change this index of one here to be an index of zero. And then we need to do this all the way down and touch every item in the array list.

Also, if we add an item at the index of zero, we bring that 11 back. We need to change this index. And then we have to do this all the way down to make room. For that 11 to be at the index of zero. So in this case, n is the number of items in the array list. And because we had to touch each item, that means this is O of n. So it is o of one on this end when you're adding something or removing something. But it is O of N to do those things on this end.

So when we remove that item, we have to re-index everything here. If we bring it back, we have to do all the re-indexing again to bring it back. So it's o of one on one end, it's O of N on the other. So if we say my list dot add at the index of one, the number 99. That's going to be this index here. We're going to drop the number 99 in here. What we have to do here is reindex this item and make that a two. And this a three and that a four. So you have to touch all of the items that come after where the insert happened.

So that is O of N. So you might say that this should be O of one half n, because on average this insert is going to be somewhere in the middle and you'll only have to touch half the items. However, there are two problems with that logic. First, big O measures worst case, not average case. But even if O of one half N was our worst case, one half is a constant and we drop constants. So likewise, when we go to remove that item, we're going to have to re-index everything that comes after it again.

So it doesn't matter if it's an insert or a remove, it is O of n. So now let's look at finding something by value. Let's say we're looking for the value of seven. We'd start at the beginning of the array list or the array. And then we say is this the value of seven. This is 11. So we keep moving until we get to the value of seven. So looking for an item by value is o of n. But it's different if we're looking for this by index. If we are looking for the index of three, we do not have to iterate all the way through this.

We can go directly to this index and that is O of one. And that is our overview of big O of a Rayless.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 2.11: Big O: Wrap Up
----------------------------------------

So now let's wrap up big O. So I'm going to bring up our graph here and say n is a hundred. Let's look at what each of these four are when n is 100. So we start on the bottom there with o of one. And that's going to equal one. O of log n is going to be approximately seven. O of N is going to be 100 because n is 100. And O of n squared is 10,000. So you can already see that there's a very big difference between each of these. And that O of n squared compared to the other three is very inefficient.

But this spread becomes even bigger. When n becomes larger. So let's look at what happens when n is 1000. Oh of one down at the bottom, there is going to still be one. It's not affected by N becoming larger. O of log n is approximately ten because two to the 10th power is 1024, so 1000 is pretty close to that. So we'll call that approximately ten. But notice it only went from 7 to 10, even though n went from 100 to 1000. O of n is going to be a thousand, and O of n squared goes all the way to a million.

So as n grows. N squared is going to grow very, very fast. It's very inefficient compared to any of these others. So we'll see as we get into the course, there will be situations where you do something and it's O of n squared. And you can rewrite the code and make it O of n. That is a huge increase in efficiency when you can do that. So now let's look at some terminology for all of these. O of n squared is a loop within a loop. O of N is proportional, it will always be a straight line.

O of log n. Divide and conquer. When you hear that that is O of log n and o of one is constant time. So those are our four terms for our four big O's that we'll see for most of the course. So now I want to flip over to a website. This is called big O Cheat sheet.com. At the very top of the page you have this chart. These two over here were not going to see in this course. In fact o of n factorial the one all the way to the left. There is something that you would have to intentionally write bad code to achieve.

The worst one that we're going to see in this course is going to be O of n squared, which is in the horrible category. And we'll make our way across here. O of n times log n. Remember we're going to see this in a couple of sorting algorithms. And then down here is where we want to stay with these three o of n o of log n and o of one. So below the chart if you scroll. They have common data structure operations. So you have a variety of data structures on the left there.

And this whole area is time complexity and it's broken into average on this side. Notice these all start with the Greek letter theta. And then worst on this side, this is going to be. Oh, but also over here we have space complexity. And this just has big O for space complexity, not omega or theta. And you can see that except for one, the skipped list, which we're not going to build in this course, they're all O of N. And this is one of the reasons we're going to spend much more time on time complexity than space complexity, because in the entire data structure section, all the space complexity is going to be the same.

So then if we scroll again. You have array sorting algorithms. And this has best with omega average theta worst O for time complexity. And then the space complexities are all over the place. And this is where we will talk about space complexity. And one of the things that's interesting is quicksort and merge sort up here at the top for the most part is o of n times log n, but the space complexity is not the best. Whereas you get down here we're going to build these three bubble sort, insertion sort and selection sort.

And you can see the time complexities for the most part are not good. They're O of n squared. But all three of them have a space complexity of O of one. So these three are considered to be, shall we say, primitive sorting algorithms. But from a space complexity they're great also and a best possible scenario over here. They are more efficient than quicksort or merge sort. This situation, by the way, is if you have already sorted data or almost sorted data, then it's going to be in.

And that's also the situation up here for quicksort when it has its worst possible scenario. So let's say you have sorted data or almost sorted data. And that could happen if you have a sorted list and you add one item to the end and then you're going to sort it, it is almost sorted. Or it could be that that item on the end is the highest number and it's completely sorted. Quicksort is going to be terrible in a situation like that. But bubble sort and insertion sort are going to be very good.

So these are the kinds of questions that you're going to get in an interview. This is why you have to know the big O and for that matter, the theta or the omega, in this case for the time complexity and understand the space complexity to be able to answer these questions. But also, I would encourage you to go to this website and spend some time looking this over. After you start getting an idea of how this works in the course, I will add a link to this website in the resources for this video.

And that is our wrap up for big O.

And classes can have class variables. I'm only going to have one variable for cookie, and that's going to be the color of the cookie.

A best practice is to make all of your class variables private. And then inside the class we can have methods. And the first method we create is called a constructor. This is what we'll use to create a new cookie with this cookie cutter. And the constructor method always has the name of the class. So our method is called cookie, just like our class. With this one, we're going to pass it a color and it will change the color of a particular cookie. So let's bring our cookies back here. And let's look at all of this code in IntelliJ. ----------------------------------------
LECTURE 3.2: References
----------------------------------------

In Java. There are variables that hold values and there are variables that hold references. And I'm going to compare and contrast those in this video. I'm going to start out with variables that hold values. An example would be an int Num1 holds the value 11. It does not hold a reference, it holds a value. And if you set num2 to be equal to num one, of course num2 is going to be equal to 11 as well. So I'm going to represent this in a little bit different way here without code like this.

When you say you want num2 to be equal to the same thing that num1 is equal to, you are setting it equal to the same value. But these two variables at this point are independent If you later change num one to be equal to 22, it is not going to change num two to be equal to 22, it remains 11. They are independent. Now let's compare this to a variable that holds a reference. Let's say we have a variable map one and we set it to be equal to this hash map.

Map one does not hold a value that is equal to this hash map. That variable holds an address that points to the hash map. It holds a reference. And these types of variables work in a different way. So if we set a new variable of map two to be equal to map one, I'm going to show you what does not happen is you do not get another hash map. That map two points to it does not work like this. So I'm going to remove this. When you set Map2 to be equal to map one.

Map one is going to pass a reference the address. So map two is going to point to the exact same. HashMap in memory. So if you said that you want to change this 11 for map one to be equal to 22, it changes it for map two as well, because map two is pointing to the exact same HashMap. So now I'm going to bring up another HashMap that we'll call map three. And now if you say that you want map two to be equal to map three, you're going to have map two point to the same place in memory as map three.

So now they both point to the same HashMap. So once you assign this kind of variable, it can be reassigned to another HashMap. And now let's do this one more time. We'll say that map one is equal to map two. And now map one is going to point down here as well. And notice that nothing is pointing to this HashMap anymore. And you can only get to this HashMap through a variable. And there are no variables pointing to this. Which means this is taking up space in memory, but it is unusable.

And Java has a process that runs periodically looking for this type of situation. And when it finds something like this where there are no variables pointing at it, it will be removed in a process called garbage collection. So now all three of these variables are pointing at this new HashMap. And the reason that this is important is when we get to the next data structure linked lists, we're going to create what are called nodes. And all of these principles with references will apply to those nodes.

So instead of having map1, map2 and map3 pointing to a hash map, you could have node one, two and three pointing at a node. And if you said that you wanted to change the value of node one from 22 to 33, and then you printed out the values for node two and node three, well, those would be 33 as well because they're pointing to the same node. And when you create a linked list you're going to have a variable called head. And that is going to be a variable that holds a reference.

In other words, is going to point to a place in memory where that node resides. So if you create a new variable let's say we call it temp and we set it to be equal to head. When you do that, you are passing a reference and you're making it where temp points at the same node that head is pointing to. And notice the node itself has a pointer a reference. And if you have one of these that's not pointing at a node, that means that it is null which means it is pointing to nothing.

But in a linked list, we're going to create nodes that can point one to another to another. So if we added another node it would look like this. The last node in the linked list is always going to point to null. It is what is called a null terminated list. And all of the variables in the linked list section, or the doubly linked list section, or stacks or queues or binary search trees. All of those nodes. Any variable that refers to that node. You can think of as just pointing at the node.

So let's say we want to have head point at the 44 node. What you would have to do in that situation is set head to be equal to another reference that is pointing at the 44 node. And there's only one thing on the screen here that is pointing at the 44 node. And it is this arrow. So if you set head to be equal to the address that is stored there, it would cause head to point to this node. So it's very important to understand that these are all just references and how it works.

When you set one reference to be equal to another reference. And you might be wondering, why would you ever want head to point to the second node? Well, you would want to do that if you were removing the first node. And this will be one of the things that we do when we get to the linked list section is learn how to remove the first node. And of course we could also remove this node. So what happens to head if you remove all of the nodes from the linked list?

Because you could have an empty linked list. That would mean that head is equal to null, which just means that head is not pointing at a node. So it is important to understand with any of the data structures where we have a node class, that any variable that is set equal to a node is not holding a value, it is holding an address that points at a node. All of those types of variables are going to hold references.

SECTION 4: Linked Lists

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 4.1: Linked List: Intro
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to look at linked lists. This will be the first data structure we build in the course. And I'm going to start out explaining linked lists by comparing them. To an array list and talk about the differences between an array list and a linked list. And an array list is the data structure that linked lists are most commonly compared to. And the reason why linked lists are more commonly compared to an array list is because linked lists are dynamic in length, just like array lists, whereas arrays are fixed in length.

So the first difference between an array list and a linked list is we're not going to have these indexes where we can go directly to a particular item. So I'm going to remove those. Another difference is that with a linked list, instead of all of these being in a contiguous place in memory, they are going to be spread out. Now, one of the things that I like to do when we move to a new data structure is change how we represent it visually. We represented the array list and arrays as green squares.

In a linked list, I'm going to represent these as purple circles. And that helps us to visually separate the two data structures. Linked list is also going to have a variable called head that points to the first node, and one called tail that points to the last node. And if you'll recall from our pointer video, both of these variables are pointers that just point to nodes. And then each node is going to have a pointer that points to the next node and the next, and so on until we get to the end.

That last node has a pointer as well. It just points to null, which means it doesn't point to anything, but it does have a variable there for that pointer. So the length list, if you look at this in memory, is all over the place like this, with each one pointing to the next to the next. So if you compare this to an array list or an array, those data structures are going to be in a contiguous place in memory like this. And that's why with those data structures, we can have indexes.

Okay. And that is our quick overview of linked lists and how they compare to array list.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 4.2: LL: Big O
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to talk about link lists big O. So what I'm going to talk about is the big O of each of the methods that we're going to create in this course. So for example we have a linked list. If we're going to add something to the end, the steps that we would do is we're going to have this arrow point to it, tail is going to point to it and it gets added into the linked list. Now from a big O perspective n is the number of nodes. So the steps that we just walked through are going to be the same.

If the linked list has ten items in it, or it has a thousand items in it, it's not going to change based on whatever n is. Which means that adding something to the end is O of one. Now this is different. When we remove the item, it looks like it would be pretty simple to pop that off, have tail point over to there and it would be an easy process. But in order to move tail over to point at that seven node. You have to have this pointer set equal to something else that is pointing at that seven node.

And there's only one thing that is pointing at that node. And it is this pointer here. And we can't move backwards to the list. We can only go the direction that the arrows are going. So let's move tail back and look what we need to do. We need to start at the head and iterate through the list like this. Until we get to this pointer. Then we can say set tail equal to that pointer. And then it moves back. But because we had to touch every node and iterate through the list.

This is O of N. So now let's look at adding an item to the other end. So we're going to have the four node point to the first node in the list. So in other words we're going to set the pointer on the four node to be equal to head like this. Then we're going to move head over to point at the new node. And that adds that into the list. And it doesn't matter how big the list is, it's going to be the same number of operations to add this item. So that is O of one.

Likewise, if we're going to remove that four node, that first one, we have a fixed number of steps to do this. We'll need to move that head pointer over one. So we'll set head to be equal to head dot next. And that moves that over. Then we'll remove this node from the list like that. And that since it's a fixed number of steps is O of one. Now let's look at inserting a node. Let's say we're going to insert this node after the 23 node. We're going to insert this at the index of three.

In order to do that we'll have to start at the head and iterate through this list and get to this node. We want the four node to point to the seven node. So this pointer points to the seven node. We'll set the new node pointer equal to that. Then we'll have the pointer from the 23 point to the new node. And that adds that into the list. But because we had to iterate through the list this is o of n. So now let's look at removing that node. So to get to that node we have to start at the head.

And we have to iterate through the entire list to get to this node. And let's just have that come down here. We want that 23 to point to the seven node and the four points there. So we'll set the pointer for the 23 to be equal to that. And then we'll set this pointer equal to null. And that removes that from the linked list. But once again we had to iterate through the list to do that. So that is o of n. The next thing is if we want to look for something by value, let's say we want to look for the value of 23.

In order to do that, we have to start at the head and iterate through and see if that's 23 and that's 23. And then finally we find it. Now the other way we can look for this is by index. And if we're looking for this as the index of two, we're going to start at the head and go 012. So it doesn't matter if we're looking for it by value or by index. It is O of n. So this is one of the places where an array list is better than a linked list. If you look for it by index and a linked list, you have to iterate through to get to that index.

So now I'm going to remove this, and I'm going to bring in a chart to compare linked lists to array lists. So everything that's in gray is the same for both. But there are a couple of these that are better for an array list, removing the last item and looking something up by index. But there are also a couple of things that are going to be better for a linked list prepending an item or removing the first item. So in other words, adding or removing something from the beginning, an array list is going to be O of n because you have to re-index everything, whereas a linked list because you don't have indexes in there, it is o of one.

So I will create a PDF of this table and attach it to this lesson. So you can download that. But that is linked list big O.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 4.3: LL: Under the Hood
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to look at linked lists under the hood. So if we have a linked list and we're going to add this node to the end and we have this node point to the new node and tail point to the new node, and we add this into the list. What is really going on when we do that. So in order to understand this we have to first understand what a node is. So this entire thing is the node. It is both the value and the pointer. It is both things. So a good way of thinking of what a node is is to think of this like a hash map.

It is not technically a hash map, but it pretty much works the way a set of nested hash maps would work. So if we look at our node this way and we're going to add this to our linked list, remember that the seven node is also basically a hash map. So how do we have that next pointer point to this new node. Well we have it point to the new node by just setting it equal to the new node. So if you'll recall from our pointers video, when we set something equal to a hash map, the variable is just pointing to the hash map.

So that is how you have one of these nodes point to the next node. So the same is going to be true of this 23 node and the three node and so on. And that very first node up at the top will have hed be set equal to it. And remember that just makes it where head is pointing to that. And then we'll have tail point to this one over here. So conceptually we can represent the linked list like this. It's a lot easier to understand as a diagram. But it is also important to understand that it's really something more like this.

Under the hood.

LECTURE 5.2: LL: Has Loop
----------------------------------------

So our next coding exercise is called has loop. And it's going to have a lot of similarities to the last coding exercise. So I'm going to bring up a linked list. And we want to check to see if this linked list has a loop. And you can see by looking at this that this does not have a loop. So I'm going to first show you how the code works. If it does not have a loop just like we had in the last video. We're going to have slow and fast. And as we start traversing the linked list, if this has a loop, you will eventually get to the point where slow and fast are going to point at the same node.

So after the first iteration of the loop, fast moves two and slow moves one. And this will be the first time that we check to see if they're equal and they're not. So we're going to go until these two are equal or until we get to the end of the linked list. So we'll move fast forward two and slow forward one. And having fast point to the last node is one of the things that breaks us out of the while loop. And since fast and slow were never equal to each other, that means we do not have a loop.

So we will return false if we have an even number of nodes. We'll move fast forward two, slow forward one, and then we do that. Check to see if they're equal. We run the loop again and move fast forward two and slow forward one. And now we're going to break out of the loop. And slow and fast were never equal to each other. So we return false because it is not true that this has a loop. So now let's look at a situation where we do have a loop, and something would have had to have gone wrong with our code to have this situation happen.

So we're going to run the loop again. Now with this situation I'm going to show slow moving first and then fast moving second. It doesn't really matter the order that you do it in, because you're doing the comparison after you have moved both so slow and fast are not equal to each other. And we have not reached the end of the linked list. So we'll keep going. We'll move, slow up one and then fast up two and do the comparison again. And then we'll move slow to here and fast to here and do the comparison.

They are not equal to each other. And then we'll move slow up here and then fast up here. And now slow and fast are pointing to the same node. And that means that we have a loop. So if this situation happens, we will return. True. Because it's true that this linked list has a loop. So I want you to notice that in this video and in the previous video, I did not include any code. And that's going to be the case for all of the interview slash leetcode exercises.

So in these coding exercises I'll give you strategies, I'll give you the visuals, but it'll be up to you to figure out how to write the code. And the reason for that is we've already covered linked lists in detail. And once you have these strategies at this point you should be able to implement them. But that is our overview of has loop.

LECTURE 5.4: LL: Remove Duplicates
----------------------------------------

Our next coding exercise is called Remove Duplicates. So if you have this linked list you can see that we have two ones and two twos. And our job is to remove the duplicates. Now there are a couple of ways of solving this problem. There's the really efficient way which uses a set and then the inefficient way that uses nested loops. If you use a set, it's O of N, and if you use nested loops it's O of n squared. But at this point in the course I haven't covered sets.

So I'm just going to walk through how to do this with nested loops. We'll have one loop that has a variable called current, and then another nested loop that has a variable called runner. And current is the current node that we're comparing to the rest of the nodes. And runner is going to run the length of the linked list. So runner is always going to check the next node. So right now it's checking to see if that two node is equal to the one node.

And I'll show you why in a moment that we have to do it that way. But then it moves up and checks to see if the three node is equal to one and it's not. Now we're checking to see if that one node is equal to one. And it is. And the reason why you need to have runner here when you're checking the next node is because we need that next pointer of the node that runner is pointing to to point to the next node. And that removes the one from the linked list.

And then we go to the end looking for ones. And we don't find any. And then we move current up one. And we set runner to be equal to current. And then we do this again. And now that two is equal to current. And we'll have the next pointer of the node that runner is pointing to, point to that five node and we remove the two node. And of course there are no other twos, and we move current up and runner back to point at the same node as current. And now we go looking for threes and move current up and runner back.

And then we look for fours. And we do this until current is past the end of the linked list. And that breaks us out of the loop. And now we've removed all of the duplicates in the linked list. And as I mentioned at the beginning of the video, there is a more efficient way of solving this with a set. And I've included both of the solutions, the one with the set and the one with the nested loops under the solution explanation tab. And that is the overview of remove duplicates.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 5.5: LL: Binary to Decimal
----------------------------------------

Now our next coding exercise is called binary to decimal. So you'll be given a linked list that looks something like this. And with binary all of the numbers are either going to be 0 or 1. So in this case this binary number with four ones would be equal to 15. But I know there's going to be a lot of people that are fairly new to binary, or they might have a basic understanding of it, but I want to talk about it a little bit deeper and talk about the pattern that we have with the numbers.

And in order to explain this, we're going to start out by treating this like a decimal number. So in decimal this would be 1111. And we know that this is worth one. This is ten. This is 100 and this is 1000. And the main thing I want to focus on here is the pattern. As we go from right to left, we start with that one and we go one to the left. That number is ten times as big. We go to the left again, and that 100 is ten times as big as the ten. And then of course, the thousand is ten times as big as the 100.

And the reason for that is each digit has ten possibilities. It's zero through nine. And because there are ten possibilities, this goes up a multiple of ten each time. And you can take all of these and put them together. And that gives you the number 1111. And let's put this back and let's say the one inch the 100th spot. We're going to change that to a zero. This becomes a zero and this becomes 1011. So now let's look at this in binary. And the main thing I'm wanting you to get is the pattern.

Because we're going to need to use this in our algorithm to solve this. So these are the numbers in binary and in binary. Instead of going zero through nine you're only going zero through one. There are only two combinations. So as we move from right to left the one doubles and becomes a two. The two doubles and becomes a four. And the four doubles and becomes an eight. And if you go through and add all of these up, they add up to 15. So it might look like all we need to do is have something that points to the first node and we take eight plus four is 12, plus two is 14 plus one is 15.

But here is the problem. You will not be given the length of the linked list, and you're not allowed to iterate through and calculate the length. So when you start here, you have no idea that this is an eight. This could be a 16, it could be a two. You're not going to have any idea of the actual value of this node. So I'm going to put this back. And so what you might want to do here is pause the video and see if you can figure out how to do this.

So now I'm going to walk through how to solve this. When current is pointing to the first node. We're just going to assume that the linked list has one node. And in this case that node has a one. So we'll say that the entire linked list is worth one. But then we're going to iterate current and go to the second node. Well, if the linked list has two nodes, that means that first node was actually worth two, not one. And we had stored this one up in the upper right.

That means we need to take that one and double it, because that's what we had stored for the first node. And then we're going to add the value of the current node. So we're going to add the one of the current node. And now it's three. And you can see that we have a two and a one. And that adds to three. But then we go to the next node. And we had stored this three to represent the first two nodes. But you can see that they are worth four and two now which add to six.

So we need to double that 3 to 6 and then add this one and make it seven. So the first three nodes are adding up to seven so far. But we have a fourth node. Now these three nodes are adding to 14. So we need to take that seven and double it to 14. And then we'll add the one and it's 15. So I want to emphasize something here. I'm going to remove this last one. And remember the linked list was worth seven at this point. If we were to add a node that was a zero, we would still take all of these and double them and make them 14.

But we're going to add the actual value that's in the node in this case zero. And in this situation this linked list would be worth 14. And that is the overview of the strategy for how to solve binary to decimal.

LECTURE 6.3: DLL: Remove Last

So now we're going to create our remove last method. And this one is very different than what we saw with a singly linked list. So with the singly linked list, in order to move tail back one, we had to start at the head and iterate through the entire linked list to get to this node, and then set tail equal to this node. And that made it o of n. And the reason is that all of the arrows point forward. So we had no way of going backwards. But with a doubly linked list, we do have arrows going the other way.

So now let's look at how we do this with a doubly linked list. So since we will be returning that last node, we will need a variable that is pointing to it that will be temp. Then we can say that tail. Equals tail dot previous. And that moves that over. And tail dot next equals null. That breaks it off like that. And then temp dot previous equals null. And then we can say return temp. So we have a couple of edge cases here. One is when we only have one item in the linked list.

We saw this with the remove last method with singly linked lists. It will be the same situation here. And then of course the other edge case is when we don't have any items in the linked list. So if you want to code this go ahead and pause it here. Otherwise I will get into the code. So we'll call it remove last. The return type is node. Now we'll code for our situation when we have multiple items in the linked list. Then we'll say temp dot previous equals null.

That is temp dot previous equals null. And that removes this from our linked list. So now we can decrement the length by one. So let's put this in with the rest of our code like this. And that brings us to our edge case with the situation where we have one item in the linked list. Now, I'm not going to walk through all of the details of how to do this, because we walked through this in detail in the singly linked list section. I'll just bring this code in here.

But what we're going to do now is we're going to rearrange these lines of code to make it easier to read. LECTURE 7.1: DLL: Palindrome Checker
----------------------------------------

In this coding exercise, we're going to check to see if a doubly linked list is a palindrome. And usually when we're talking about palindromes we're talking about words or sentences. For example the word racecar is a palindrome. And that means it's spelled the same way forwards or backwards. But in our doubly linked list we're going to have numbers. So we're going to see if this is a palindrome. And it is. You will be given the length in this coding exercise.

And you'll need to use that to calculate how many iterations that you need to go through the doubly linked list. So we'll have variables. I'll call mine forward and backward. And we check to see if these are equal. They're both one. So that's good. And then we do it again. And because this is an odd number we don't need to check the one in the middle. So in this situation you would return true. If at any time forward and backward were not equal, you would return false that it is not a palindrome.

Of course, you can also have a doubly linked list that has an even number of nodes. And then you would check if these two are equal and these two are equal. The big thing is figuring out how to do this for an odd number of nodes, and an even number of nodes. And of course, if you only have one node that's going to be a palindrome, you would return true. And that is our introduction to the coding exercise palindrome.

LECTURE 7.3: DLL: Partition List

But when you do partition list with a doubly linked list, there's an edge case. And I want to show you that edge case. So if you remember from partition list with a singly linked list, if we want to have everything that's greater than or equal to five on one side and everything that is less than five on the other, we take all of the nodes that are less than five, and we're going to put them together like this. And then we're going to have dummy nodes and variables called previous one and previous two.

All of this is going to be the same, with a singly linked list and a doubly linked list, with the exception, of course, that we have previous pointers that we need to attach in addition to the next pointers. And then if you'll recall, we just take both of these and then combine them, but with a doubly linked list. When we go to combine these there's an edge case. And that is if the second list is empty, you're going to have to write code specifically to deal with this edge case.

And by this point in the course, you should be able to take out a piece of paper and draw this out and figure out why that's an edge case. So I'm not going to give you the details of why it's an edge case, but I am going to give you this hint that you're going to have to write code to specifically deal with this. And that is your hint to be able to solve doubly linked list. Partition list.

SECTION 8: Stacks & Queues
########################################

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 8.1: Stack: Intro
----------------------------------------

Now let's look at stacks. So my favorite analogy for a stack is a can of tennis balls. Let's go ahead and remove these and I'll add one back. This is pushing an item onto the stack. And then of course we can push a second one onto the stack. But the thing that makes a stack a stack. Is you can only get to the last item that you pushed onto the stack. That second tennis ball, as soon as you push that second one on, you can't get to the first one without first removing the second one.

And then when we push the third ball on, we can't get to the second one. The only one we can get to is the one that is on top. This is called LIFO. Last in, first out. So after we remove that third ball, it is only now that we can get to the second one and then we can get to the first one. So let's look at an example of where you might use a stack. Let's see. We have a web browser and we go to Facebook. And then after that we go to YouTube and then we go to Instagram and then we check our email.

What we have created to the right of this computer screen is a stack of all of the previous websites that we have visited. So when we hit the back button, we are popping an item off of the stack, and it is only by removing that item that we can hit the back button again and get to the next item, and so on. A common way of implementing a stack is with an array list. An array list works better than an array here, because we don't know how many items we're going to be adding to the stack, so it's better to have a dynamic data structure.

So technically we could use either end of the array list for adding and removing items. The only requirement for a stack is that we add and remove items from the same end. So if we do that at that end of the array list where I just did this, that is O of one for both adding and removing items. However, if we do this on the other end and we're going to remove this item because of all of the re-indexing we have to do, and also bringing that item back, we would have to re-index this to push something back onto the stack.

This ends up being O of N for adding and removing from the stack if we do it on that end. So if you use an ArrayList to implement your stack, you always want to do it on the end, not the beginning. Since stacks are usually represented vertically, you can think of it like this. This would be popping an item from the stack and pushing an item onto the stack. So another way to implement a stack that is common and it is the way we will implement it here is with a linked list.

And just like with an array list, you can add and remove items from either end as long as it's the same end. So in other words, your stack could look like this with the null terminated end at the bottom of the stack. Or it could look like this. So let's put this back as a linked list, and look at which one of those two is best from a big O perspective. So it is O of N to remove an item from this end and O of one to put this back on. It is actually better from a big O perspective to do it from the other end, because both removing and adding are O of one.

So when you're using a linked list for your stack, you always want it to look this way with a null terminated end at the bottom. So let's put this back this way. There are a couple of methods that we wrote for the linked list that we can leverage for our stack, and those linked list methods are remove first and prepend. Except with the stack. We're going to call that pop. And push. And then went the length list. We had head and tail. But since we represent a stack vertically, we're going to change these names to top and bottom.

But with a stack we never add or remove anything from the bottom. We do everything at the top, so we don't even need that bottom pointer. We can do this just. With top. And that is our quick introduction to stacks.

LECTURE 8.5: Queue: Intro
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to do our introduction to cues. So a cue is just like when you get in line people can get in line behind you. And then when you remove someone from the line, you take the first person in line out of the line. This is Fifo first in, first out as opposed to stacks which was last in first out. So a couple of pieces of terminology with a queue. When you add someone or something to a queue, you would say that you have in queued that person.

And then when you remove someone from a queue or a node from a queue, you would say that you have dequeued them. So now let's look at a couple of ways we can implement a queue, just like we did with stacks. We're going to first look at an array list. So the only rule with a queue is you have to enqueue and dequeue on opposite ends. So with an array list if we dequeue here or we enqueue on this end. Either way it would be o of one. But on the other end if we dequeue or enqueue on this end it's O of n.

So you could say, hey, I want to enqueue on this side and I want to dequeue on this side. You're still going to have one O of one and one O of n. Or it could be the other way. We're going to enqueue over here and dequeue over here. Either way you're going to have one side that's O of one and one side that is O of n. So let's compare this to a linked list with a linked list. To remove an item on this end is o of n, but to add an item is o of one.

Then on this end to remove an item is o of one, but also adding an item is O of one. So the one thing you don't want to do if you're going to use a linked list for a queue, is you do not want to dequeue on this end. You want to enqueue on this end and dequeue on this end. That way, enqueue and dequeue are both o of one. So with the linked list we also had head and tail. We'll rename these and call these first and last. And that is our introduction to cuz.

LECTURE 9.1: Stack: Reverse String

Then we create a new string and start popping these letters and adding them to that new string. And then we pop the next letter and the next one, and so on. And that's how we use a stack to reverse a string.

LECTURE 9.2: Stack: Parentheses Balanced
----------------------------------------

So our next coding exercise is called parentheses balanced. So you will have a string that will contain nothing but parentheses. And these parentheses are balanced. There's an open parentheses and a closing parentheses. However these are not balanced. And you might say that there's an equal number of each. So why are these not balanced. And the reason for that is with parentheses we always start with an opening parentheses and then have a corresponding closing parentheses.

This string also has balanced parentheses. They're also balanced if we do it like this. Because we can have something in a set of parentheses inside of a set of parentheses. So now let's walk through how it is that we check if the parentheses are balanced. I'm going to bring up a stack and we're going to iterate through this string. And when we see an open parentheses we're going to add it to the stack. And then we go here and add this open parentheses to the stack.

And when we see a closing parentheses we pop one of these from the stack. And this is also a closing parenthesis. And we'll pop this from the stack. And now we've gotten to the end of the string. And because that stack is empty it means that the parentheses are balanced. So now let's look at another example. We'll put this back at the beginning and move these parentheses. So we'll start out by adding that open parentheses to the stack. And then we go to here.

And then we pop that from the stack. And then with this open parentheses we add that to the stack. And then with the closing parentheses we remove that from the stack. And we have gotten to the end of the string. And the stack is empty. And that means the parentheses are balanced. So now let's put this back and remove one of the closing parentheses and see how that works. We'll add this opening parentheses to the stack. And we'll do the same thing with this one.

And then when we move to here we pop this one from the stack. We are at the end of the string. The stack is not empty. And that means the parenthesis are not balanced. And we're going to return false. In the previous two situations where the stack was empty, we would return true that the parentheses are balanced, but in this situation it is false. So let's put this back at the beginning and look at what happens when we have too many closing parentheses.

We will add this to the stack. Move to here and pop this from the stack. But when we move to here we will have a check to see. Is that stack empty? If it is ever empty before we reach the end of the string, that means our parenthesis are not balanced. So in this case we return false and that is how we do parenthesis balanced.

LECTURE 9.3: Stack: Sort Stack
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to learn how to do sort stack. So if we have a stack of numbers like this, we want to sort it where it ends up like this. So I'm going to put this back. And in order to do this we're going to have a second stack. And we're going to have a variable I'm going to call mine temp. So we'll take that top item from the first stack, pop it and set temp to be equal to that value. And if the second stack is empty we'll just push this onto that stack.

And then we do the same thing again where we pop from the first stack and put it in temp. So what we're going to want is to have the lowest numbers at the bottom of the second stack. So we're going to compare that value of one to the value of three in the second stack. And in order to do that we have a method called peek. And all that does is look at the value of the top item in the stack, and you will need to use that in your solution. And because three is greater than one, we're going to move this back to the other stack and put the one at the bottom.

So now we'll do the same thing again. Pop from the first stack put it in temp. But because three is greater than one we're going to add this to the stack. And then we go back and do this again. Four is greater than three. We'll add this to the stack. And then we'll move the two up here. And now we're going to use that same logic that we saw earlier. Four is greater than two. So we're going to put this on the other stack. Three is greater than two.

So we're going to put this one over here. And now finally we can add the two to this stack. And because temp is empty we can empty this other stack. And now our stack is sorted. And that is how we do sort stack.

LECTURE 9.4: Queue Using Stacks
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to talk about implementing a queue with stacks. And I'm only going to have one video for the next two coding exercises. And you might be wondering how do we implement a queue with stacks. So remember a queue is first in first out. So if we add an item to the queue this is the first item in the queue. This will also be the first item removed. What we cannot do when we add the second item is do this because the nature of a stack is we don't have any way of getting to that one.

So I'm going to remove this. So in order to do enqueue, which is going to be the next coding exercise, you'll pop that one from the first stack and push it onto the second stack. Add the next item and then do this in reverse. And then to add a third item we're going to do this. Add the third item and move these back. That is how we enqueue these items. And then we'll do one more. Let's move all of these over to this stack. And then we'll add a fourth item.

And then move all of these back. That's how we enqueue the number four is by moving everything to the other stack and then pushing the four on. And then moving everything back. So that's what you need to do to write the code for enqueue. And then once you do this dequeue is very easy. Remember the first item that we added to our queue was the one. And then it was the two and the three and the four. Now I'll have students ask me if this is an efficient way of implementing a queue.

And I'm going to tell you that it is not. This is a very inefficient way of doing this, but it is a common interview question to see if you can implement a queue with stacks.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 10.1: Trees: Intro & Terminology
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to look at trees. And we've already seen a tree in this course. And that was a linked list. A linked list is just a tree that doesn't fork. And we said that this node was similar to a hash map that looked like this. So what I'm going to do is take this node and make changes to it and turn it into a binary tree node. So the first thing I'm going to do is change the color. Since we're moving to a new data structure, and instead of pointing to next, this points to left and right.

So if we're going to use the hash map analogy again you could say that it is something like this. So let's turn this back into the graphic. We'll shrink this down. That left and right pointer can point to new nodes like this. And this would be similar to a set of nested hash maps that look like this. So let's put this back. And the way we've built these because it can only point to left and right. It can only point to two other nodes. That's what makes it a binary tree.

But there is no rule that says that a tree has to be a binary tree. You can have each node point to three nodes or ten nodes or 100 nodes. It's just the way we're doing it here. Each node can only point to two nodes. And then of course, these new nodes that we've added can also point to two other nodes themselves. So now let's look at some terminology. This tree here is what is called full and a full tree. Every node either points to zero nodes or two nodes.

So if you do something like this. Now you have a node, the seven node that only points to one node, so it is no longer full. So let's remove that. Make it full again. And if we remove these two, it is still full, but it is also something else. It is perfect. With a perfect tree. Any level in the tree that has any nodes is completely filled all the way across, so this is also a perfect tree. But if we remove these two, it is no longer perfect. But it is still full.

So let's bring these two back. So a perfect tree is also something else. It is complete. So with a complete tree, you are filling the tree from left to right with no gaps. So if we add a node down here, it is still complete because we are filling the tree from left to right. But it is no longer full and it is no longer perfect. If we add one more node like this, filling this from left to right, it remains complete, but it is now also full. But it is not perfect because we haven't filled it all the way across.

So if we do this now, it is full and perfect and complete. Okay. So let's focus in on this tree and drop some of these off. So this node here is a parent. Of these two child nodes. These two child nodes, because they share the same parent, are also called siblings. And every node can only have one parent. So if you see something like this where a node has more than one parent, then it is not a tree. So let's put this back. And child nodes, of course, can also be parent nodes.

Now these nodes at the bottom, they don't have any children. And a node that doesn't have children is called a leaf. All right. That is our intro for trees.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 10.2: Binary Search Trees: Example
----------------------------------------

So the binary tree we looked at in the last video was a binary tree, but it wasn't a binary search tree. In order for it to be a binary search tree, the nodes have to be laid out in a particular way. So let's take a look at that. So let's say we have one node in our tree. And we're going to add another node with a binary search tree. If the number is greater than it's going to go on the right of that node. And if it's less than it's going to go on the left of that node.

And because 76 is greater than we're going to place it there. Now let's take a look at another node. The 52 is greater than 47. And we're always going to start by comparing it to the node at the top. And because it's greater than we're going to go to the right. But there's already a node there. So what we're going to do is we're going to compare that 52 to the 76. And because it's less than 76. That goes on the left of that node. So let's look at another node.

At 21. We're always going to start at the top. And because the spot to the left of the 47 is open. We'll place it right there. So let's just look at a few more here. We'll say 82. We'll start at the 47. It's greater than we'll go to the right. We'll compare it to the 76. It's greater than again in this case that spot is open. So we'll place it. 18, it is less than 47. So we go to the left and we look at the 21 is less than the 21 that spots open.

So we'll place that there. And one final node we'll start at the 47. It's less than we go left to the 21. 27 is greater than the 21. So we place that on the right. So a couple of things to point out about a binary search tree. If you take any node in the binary search tree, all nodes below it to the right are going to be greater than that node. Everything on the left is going to be less than. And that is our overview. Of binary search trees.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 10.3: BST: Big O
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to look at binary search tree. Big O in order to do this we'll have to do some simple math. So the number of nodes in this tree right now obviously is one. But you could also write this as two to the one minus one. Two to the one is two. Minus one equals one. And you'll see why I'm doing this here in a second. But if we have a second level like this, this is two to the two minus one, two to the two is four, minus one is three.

Two to the three minus one, two to the four minus one. Now, as we get into very large numbers here, that one is insignificant. So let's remove this and we'll say this is approximately two to the four nodes. This is approximately two to the three and two to the two and two to the one. Now, if you're going to look for a node in this tree, it's going to take. One step, obviously. But when we go to two levels, let's say we're going to look for the number 76.

That is one two steps. So we have two to the two nodes approximately. And it took us two steps to find something. Let's say we're going to look for the number 27. It is one, two, three steps. And if we were going to look for the number 49, in this case it would be one, two, three, four steps. We have two to the four nodes and it took four steps. So it's four steps to find something. It's four steps to remove something because you have to iterate through the list, find the node and then remove it.

And it's also four steps if we're going to add a node, if we're going to add a node under that 49, we would have to iterate through four steps and then we could insert it. So that means that all of these are o of log n and o of log n is very efficient. So remember O of log n is achieved by doing divide and conquer. So let's bring our tree back here and show how it is that we're doing that. So if we're going to look for that 49 node again, we start at the top.

And then we go, right? And when we do, we just made it where we never have to look at anything that's on the left of the 47. So we are effectively removing these from the search. And that's not a big deal when you have a small tree like this. But if you had a million items in the tree, you just made it where there are half a million items that you don't have to look at. And then from here we're going to go left. And now we have made it where half of the remaining nodes don't need to be looked at like that.

And then we finally come down to the 49 node. And we have done that again. And that is divide and conquer. So let's bring all of this back. So for our examples for calculating time complexity, so far we have used a perfect tree. And a perfect tree is going to give you your best possible scenario. And remember we measured best possible scenario with Omega. You're more likely to see a tree that looks like this. But even in this situation, you would say that this is roughly log in.

But now let's look at our worst case. Our worst case would be if we had a node, and then the next one was greater than. And it goes to the right, and the next one is greater than that. And it goes to the right of that one. And the next one is greater than that. And it just keeps going on and on in a straight line. If the tree never forks, it is essentially. A linked list. So let's say we want to go look for the 91 node. How many steps would it take?

It would be one, two three, four. And we have four nodes in the tree that is O of n. So the big O of a binary search tree technically is O of n, not O of log n. So let's bring our other nodes up here like this. What we assume with a binary search tree is it's not going to be a straight line. We're not going to have that worst possible scenario. And we treat it as if it is O of log n. We don't treat this like an O of n data structure. So for lookup, insert and remove we treat this as if it is O of log n.

So now let's take all three of these the lookup, the insert, the remove and compare it to a linked list. So look up in a linked list. If we're going to look up the number 91, we would have to iterate through the entire list until we found that number, which makes this O of n. Which makes a binary search tree better at this. So now let's look at remove. If we're going to remove that number 91, whether we're going to look it up by index, it's at the index of three, or if we're going to look it up by value.

Either way, we have to iterate through the linked list until we get to this node. And then we can remove it. And that is O of n. But now let's look at insert C with a linked list. There is no advantage to keeping it sorted. We're not able to find anything faster because it's sorted. When we looked for the 91, we still had to iterate through the list. So for insert we would just add an item to the end. And that is O of one. So insert is actually faster with a linked list.

So let's look at all three of these side by side with a linked list and a binary search tree. Insert is faster for a linked list. And that's because we can just add an item to the end and it's O of one. We're inserting an item in a binary search tree is O of log n. Lookup is faster with a binary search tree because it's over log in instead of O of N, and then removing an item is also faster with a binary search tree. And all of our comparisons here also apply to an array list, and I'll use an array list instead of an array for our example.

Because linked lists and binary search trees are both dynamic sized data structures, so I think this is a better comparison. So if you're keeping your array list unsorted the way we are with our linked list, you would just add an item to the end. That's O of one that's going to be better than a binary search tree. But if you're looking up or removing by value, not by index, but by value, it's going to be O of n. And that is our overview of binary search tree big O.

SECTION 12: Hash Tables
