----------------------------------------
LECTURE 12.1: Hash Table: Intro
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to do our introduction to hash tables. I'm going to bring up an address space here. And we're going to create this address space by just creating an array. And then in this array we're going to store key value pairs. So what I'm going to do here is I'm just going to do the inventory for a hardware store. So one of the things that we would have in inventory would be nails. So this is what's referred to as a key value pair. So nails is the key.

And then a thousand would be the value. So when a couple of videos we will be writing the code for a hash method. I'm just going to represent that graphically here. Like this. And what we're going to do is we're going to run the hash on the key and the key value pair. So every letter has an Ascii text numerical value. So what we're going to do is run a calculation on all the numbers that are associated with these letters. So we'll run this through the hash.

So first we are going to get our key value pair back. The hash is only run on the key and gives us an address. That address is going to be one of the indexes in the array. And that's where we store our key value pair. So now let's look at a couple of characteristics of this hash method. First it is one way. So when you have a key like nails, and you run this through the hash method and you get an address, the thing that you cannot do is do it the other way and run two through this and get nails.

It is only one way. The other thing is that the hash method is deterministic, and what that means is every single time we run nails through this, we will always get the same address. So this hash method is both of these things one way and deterministic. So let's bring back our address space here and just look high level how we would write methods to use this hash method. So we will create a set method where it takes a key and a value. And let's say we pass this nails and 1000.

When we run this through here we're going to get our key value pair. But the hash method is also going to give us an address where we're going to store this. So we'll put that up there. And now let's do a few more of these. We'll say set screws. We run this through here. We get an address and a key value pair. And that goes at six. And then we'll say nuts. And we'll run that through the hash and we get the address of two. So in this situation you get what is called a collision because you already have something at the index of two.

So what you don't want to do is just overwrite this. You want to be able to keep both. So there are multiple ways of storing multiple items at a particular address. And we'll talk about that in some more detail in an upcoming video. So let's just add one more. So we have a more filled out hash table like this. So now that we've set a bunch of these, let's look at an example of our Get method. So if we say get bolts we can run bolts through this same hash method.

And get the address. Remember that hash method is deterministic, which means if you put bolts in there, you will always get the index of four. And then we'll write our get method to return the value that's associated with that key value pair. It'll be a little bit more complex if we have something like this. Get nails. We run this through here, we get that. Number two, we have multiple items here. So we have to have a way of going through all of the keys that are going to be at a particular index, and then returning the value associated with that particular key.

And that is our quick overview of hash tables.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 12.2: HT: Collisions
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to talk about a couple of ways that we can deal with collisions. So I'm going to bring up our address space here and put some key value pairs in here. One of the ways of dealing with collisions that we saw in the last video is that you just put the next key value pair at the same address, even if there's already one there. This method is called separate chaining. So now let's remove that key value pair we just added. And look at another way that we might do this.

Another common way of doing this is if there is an item already at the address where you want to put something, you just go to the next open spot and put it there. And then if you're going to add another item, you just keep going and looking for an open spot until you find one, and then you place it there. This is called linear probing. And this is one of the types of open addressing. So separate chaining and linear probing are the most common way of dealing with collisions.

The way we're going to deal with collisions in this course is going to be separate chaining. That's going to be where we have all of these at one address. And the way we're going to do that is by having a linked list at each one of these addresses. So a linked list is a common way of implementing separate chaining and putting multiple items at a particular address. And that is how we are going to deal with collisions.

Or you could just leave the prime number out altogether. And then comes the part where the real magic happens. We're going to use modulo and then data map dot length. And remember data map dot length in this case is seven. Our array has a length of seven. So when we say modulo seven what it's doing is it's giving you the remainder.

If you did division. So anything that you divide by seven is going to have a remainder of zero at the lowest end and six as the highest number remainder that you can have. So this will make it where our range is zero through six, and our address space just so happens to be zero through six. So this equation is always going to return a number that is one of the indexes in this array. So then after we go through this for loop for all of those characters at the end of this we will return hash.

And when we do return that hash, it is always going to be a number that is zero through six. And that is all of our code. For our hash method.

LECTURE 12.8: HT: Big O
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to look at the big O of hash tables. And I'm also going to bring up our graphic for our hash method. And because this hash method has to be run every time we add an item or we get an item, we have to determine what is the big O of the hash method itself. Well, the hash method itself is O of one. And the reason for that is the hash method is going to go through the same number of operations to determine a hash, regardless of the number of items that are already in the hash table.

Now let's walk through setting an item. Say we're going to set washers. We would run it through our hash method. And we're going to say in this case that it gives us an index of zero. And then we'll add that item like that. It is a single step. So you can see where that would be considered O of one. Now how about getting an item? We'll get screws. We'll run this through our hash method. And that gives us an index of six. And we find screws in one step.

But what if our hash table doesn't look like this? What if our hash table looks like this? Now, when we say get screws, it's going to be at the index of two. And then we have to iterate through this entire linked list until we finally get to screws. So you might think that that makes this a within. But when you're dealing with a hash table, the assumption is going to be that that hash method is going to create very random addresses in your hash table is going to look something more like this.

You'll have a larger address space. You'll have a very good hash method that will randomize where these things are put. And because of that, we treat getting an item and setting an item and a hash table. As of one. And that is hash table big O.

LECTURE 12.9: HT: Interview Question
----------------------------------------

In this video, we're going to go over a very common interview question that involves arrays and hash tables. And there are different variations on this question, but I'll show you one version. So I'm going to bring up a couple of arrays like this. And the objective is to see if the two arrays have an item in common. And obviously they both contain five. But how do you write the code to do that. So this is pretty simple to do with some nested for loops.

But the problem is if you have nested for loops it's very inefficient. So that is not the answer they're looking for. But so we can compare the two solutions. Let's start out with the inefficient solution and look at how that works. So first you would create a for loop to go through the first array. So you get this first item. Then you have a second nested for loop that compares that value to all of the values in the second array. Then you move that first array over to the next one and compare it to every single value in the second array.

And then we do it again until we finally find our match. So in this situation the arrays are three items long and we had nine operations. So that is O of n squared. So let's go quickly through the code to do this. I'm going to shrink this down. And we're going to call this method item in common. We're going to pass it the two arrays. We'll call them array one and array two. And then we'll return a boolean true if there is a match and then false if there is not.

So we'll start out with this for loop here. So if you haven't seen a for loop like this before this just is going to loop through array one. And then I is going to be equal to the value at each index. So it'll first be equal to one and then three and then five. And then we'll have another for loop that does the same thing on array two. And if these are ever equal we'll return true if we go through the for loops and we don't find any matches then we'll return false.

Now let's flip over and take a look at this code in IntelliJ. So that is the code that we just wrote right there. And then I put our main method inside of the same class. So down here we have our two arrays. Array one and array two with the values that we just saw. And then down here we're going to run item in common with array one and array two. And in this case we should get true. So I'll run this. And you can see up here we get true. And then I'm going to come down here to array two.

And I'm going to replace this five with a six. And now when we run this we should get false because we don't have any matches. So I'll run this again. And you can see that we get false. So this code does work. It's just really inefficient because it has the nested for loops. So now let's flip back. So now let's look at how we can do this much more efficiently with a hash table. So we'll bring our hash table in like this. So now what we're going to do is we're going to loop through that first array.

And what we're going to do is add these values as keys. Remember these are key value pairs. And the value. Is going to be true. And then we'll do the same thing for the three. We'll have that value be true. And then we'll have the same thing for the five. So then we'll have a separate for loop. But this will not be a nested for loop to go through the second array. So we'll check to see if that value of two is on our hash table. And that is an O of one operation to do that comparison.

And then we'll do the same thing for the for and the same thing for the five. And of course that is in the hash table. And in that case, we'll have our method return true. But if you look what we did here, we ran through each array. One time. We looped through the first one straight through and then the second one straight through. So we've said before that N was three. So this would be O of two n. But remember we drop constants. So now this is O of n.

So doing it this way is much more efficient. O of n is much more efficient than O of n squared. So now let's look at how we would code this. Now the way this starts out, this line of code looks exactly the same. But we'll start this out by creating a hash table. Technically, this is going to be a hash map, but a hash map is a hash table. And we'll call this my hash map. That will create a for loop, just like we did before, to loop through array one.

But inside of this for loop, we're going to put the value from each item in the array. That will be the key, and then the value will be true in the key value pair. That will have a second for loop, where we just check to see if J is in our HashMap as a key. If there is ever a match, we'll return true. If we go through this loop and we don't find a match, we'll return false. So now let's flip over and take a look at this code in IntelliJ. So there is our new item and common method there.

So one of the things I wanted to point out down here in our main method, this is all the same down here. So when we call the item in common method and pass it array one and array two, you're calling this method exactly the same way the method is written in a different way. But to the end user that might use this method, they are calling it exactly the same way as they did before. So you can see here that array one and array two do have an item in common.

They both have a five. So we would expect this to return true. So I'll run this. And you can see that it returns true. And then I'll come down here and change this five in array, two to a six. And now when we run this. It returns false. So the key takeaway here is that either way that you write this, it will work. But can you get this done and make it O of N is the real interview question.

And we come up to the hash map. And s is equal to three. So that is not a unique character. And then we get to the U. Well now the U only has a quantity of one. So we know that that is the first unique character in this string. And then we will return that character. If you have a situation like this where you don't have any unique characters, you would just return null.

And that is how you can use a hash map to determine the first non-repeating character.

----------------------------------------

So the next exercise is going to be subarray sum. And what we're going to do is find the subarray whose numbers total a particular target number. In this case we'll use the target of nine. And the reason is that as we are iterating across the array we're going to see these three numbers first.

And then what you'll return is an array with the indexes that are the beginning and the end of the subarray. And those will be returned in an array one and three. And one of the variables that we'll use I call it currentsum. You could call this running total. So what I mean by this is at this point in the array current sum is one. And then after moving to here the current sum is three because it's one plus two is three plus three is six plus four is ten plus five is 15. So that's what I mean by current sum. And during each iteration we're going to take current sum minus the target. So in this case current sum is one minus nine is negative eight.

And we're going to look in the hash map to see if there's a negative eight in the hash map, and obviously the hash map is empty. So there is not. So we're going to add current sum and the index to the hash map. So that means at the index of zero the current sum was one. And then we moved to the next number. And then we do the same thing. We take the current sum minus the target is negative six. And we look in the hash map to see if there's a negative six.

And there's only a one. So we put the current sum of three and the index of one. And then we move to the next number. At this point current sum is six minus nine is negative three. And we check the hash map to see if there's a negative three in there. And there is not. So we add the current sum and the index to the hash map. And then we move to the next item. At this point current sum is ten, minus nine is one, and one is in the hash map at the index of zero.

And what that means if that index is zero in the hash map that our subarray starts with, the next index and the end of the subarray is going to be where I is pointing the index of three. So now we have the information to be able to return this array of one and three the beginning and ending index of the subarray that equals the target. So now I'm going to put everything back the way it was like this. And I'm going to make a change to the array. I'm going to move the one to the end.

And this brings us to the one edge case that I need to show you. And now our range starts at the index of zero. And this is a particular edge case starting at the index of zero. To deal with this, we have to pre-populate the HashMap with this, with a current sum of zero and an index of negative one. Now obviously arrays don't have negative indexes. We're only putting this in the HashMap. But I am going to put this in the array. And like I said obviously arrays don't have negative indexes.

But I'm just going to put that there as a visual. And as we work through this you'll see why it is that we need to do this. So we're going to start at the index of zero. That value of two. So current sum is two minus the target is negative seven. That is not in the hash map. So we'll add this to the hash map that the current sum was two at the index of zero. And then we move to here and current sum is five minus nine is negative four. Negative four is not in the hash map.

So we'll put the current sum of five in the hash map that is at the index of one. So then we move to here. And now current sum is nine and the target is nine and nine minus nine is zero. So you can see why we have to have a zero in the hash map. When you go to look for that it has to be there. And it says that the index for that is negative one. And the beginning of the range of our subarray will be the index after that, which is the index of zero.

And then the ending index is going to be whatever I is. So the array to be returned will contain zero and two. And that is how we do subarray sum.

LECTURE 13.7: Set: Remove Duplicates
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to move on to sets. And if you're not familiar with sets, right before this video I included an article explaining some of the details about sets. And this is a very common application for a set. We're going to start with an array list. And then we're going to pass the array list as an argument to when we create a set. And one of the characteristics of a set is that it does not allow duplicates. You'll end up with a set that looks like this.

And ultimately what we want is an array list with all of the duplicates removed. So we'll take the set and then pass that as an argument to creating a new array list. Now I want you to notice that the array list and the set are in square brackets, but they are not the same data structure. Sets do not allow duplicates and they don't have indexes. One of the other characteristics of a set is when you look up by value. Let's say we're going to look up the value four in this set.

That is an O of one operation. Whereas if we look up the value of four in this array list, we have to iterate all the way through the array list looking for a four. That's O of n. So sets are O of one to look up by value and array lists are O of n to lookup by value. And that is our quick overview for remove duplicates.

LECTURE 13.8: Set: Has Unique Chars
----------------------------------------

In this video, we're going to write code to check to see if a string contains only unique characters. So let's consider the string success, and this one does not contain all unique characters. We have multiple S's and multiple C's. And we're going to iterate over this and put each of these letters in a set. We'll check to see if the set contains an S and it does not at this point. So we'll add that. And then we iterate over to the you check to see if a u is in the set.

There is not. So we'll add that and then we'll move to the C. And because the set does not contain a C we'll add this. And now we're going to move to this c. And the reason why a set is better than an array list in this situation is that all of these lookups are O of one. If you use an array list instead of a set to look for the letter C, you would have to iterate over all of these to find that C, but with a set, that's not the case. We can go directly to that character as an O of one operation.

And when we do, and we see that C there, that means that this string does not contain unique characters. So in this case you want the method to return false. If you got to the end of this and you didn't have any matches in the set, that means all of the characters are unique. And in that case you would return true. And that's how you most efficiently check to see if a string has unique characters.

There's two and four, and there is one and five. I'm going to start out by showing you the inefficient way of doing this. And that would be with a nested loop.

If we had a nested loop, we would start with the first item up here and then look for the corresponding number, the number that you would add to this to equal six. And the corresponding number of course would be five. So we would check to see is this a five. It is not. Is this a five. That's not a five either. Is this a five? Well, now we have found the corresponding number, and we know that we have a one and a five that can equal six. But we have touched every item in that second array.

And then the nested loop will go to here. And then look at every item again. So I'm not going to go through all of the steps here. But it is much more efficient to use a set. Now we can iterate through this array and looking up values in that set looking for that corresponding three is an O of one operation.

And then we move to here and the corresponding number is two. And looking up that number two is o of one It is much more efficient than using that nested loop. So what we'll do with this pair is put them in an array two and four. Then we move to the next item. And that second array. We look for the corresponding number which is one, and create another array with one and five. And these are the two combinations that add up to that target of six. And what we're going to return is an array list that contains these two arrays.

And that's going to look like this. This is what we are going to return. So I want to emphasize this is not an array list with nested array list. This is an array list that contains two arrays. And that is how we most efficiently find pairs.

LECTURE 13.10: Set: Longest Consecutive Sequence

Now we're going to look at how we determine what is the longest sequence in an array. And specifically this is going to be the longest consecutive sequence. And the thing that really threw me the first time I saw this problem is the numbers don't need to be sequential in the array. It just needs to contain the consecutive numbers. So in this case 123, four, which means that the longest consecutive sequence is four. It's four items. So I'm going to put these back.

And to make this more efficient because we're going to be doing a lot of looking up by value. We're going to use the array to create a set. And remember sets don't have indexes but they're very fast when looking up by value. So we're going to iterate over this. We'll start with this first item 100 Hundred, and the first thing we'll do with that is check to see if the number before 100 is in the set. So if it was if 99 was in the set, we would completely skip over that 100 because we want to find the first number in that sequence.

And if the next number below 100 is in the set, that means that 100 is not the first number in that sequence. I'm going to change this back to 200. And because a 99 is not in there, we're going to say that the current streak is one. And then we'll look for 101. But 101 is not in the set. So I'll put that back. And that means our longest streak that we've seen so far is one. So I'll take our current streak, put it at zero, and then we move to here and we'll check to see is the previous number to four in the set.

And it is. And we only want to work with the first number in the sequence. And we know that four is not the first number in the sequence. So we're just going to skip it. And then we go to 200 and we look for the previous number which would be 199. And 199 is not in the set, which means that 200 is the beginning of a new sequence. So I'm going to change the streak to one. And then we'll look for 201. And that is not in the set. So that one only has a sequence a streak of one.

So now I'll put the current streak back to zero. And then we'll look at the one. We'll check to see if the previous number to one is in the set, which would be zero. Zero is not in the set. So we'll change current streak to one. And now we'll see if the set has a two. It does. So current streak is two. Now we look for a three. Current streak is three. We look for a four. Current streak is four. We look for a five. And it does not contain a five.

So the streak ends at four. So we'll update the longest streak to four. And we'll put these all back and change our current streak to zero. And then we'll look at the three. Is the previous number to three. In the set. It is it's the two. So we're not going to even look at the three. And two is the previous number to two in the set. And it is. That's the one. So we're not going to look at this one. And now we've gone all the way through our set.

And our longest streak is four. And this is what you return at the end of the method. And that's how you use a set to very efficiently determine the longest sequence.

LECTURE 14.1: Graph: Intro
----------------------------------------

Okay. Now let's look at graphs. We're going to start out by bringing this up. This is called a vertex or a node. The proper way to say it is vertex but you'll hear the word node. And we'll bring up another vertex. The plural would be vertices. Then between the vertices we have what is called an edge or a connection. The proper way to say it is edge. So we have vertices and edges. Let's bring in another vertex like this. So this vertex can have an edge with this vertex, or it can have one.

With this one we're going to have one with both. There is no limit to how many other vertices that a vertex can connect to. So now that we have a small graph, let's look at a couple of concepts. Let's say you want to go from this vertex to this vertex. Well, when you look at this, you would say that you don't want to go this way because that is two hops. And this one is one you'd want to go the more direct route. But with graphs, one of the things you can have, you're not always going to have, but you can have are weighted edges.

So where you would use something like this would be in a maps app. You're using Google Maps. And the road between 76 and 82 has a lot of traffic. So you go this way instead, because this has a cost of five, and going this way has a cost of 15. Another place you'll see something like this is with network routing protocols. It would be better to have an extra router hop and have two very fast links, then to go the way with the very slow link. And like I said, the edges can be weighted or not weighted.

So another concept with graphs. We'll explain this with a Facebook connection between you and a friend. So with a typical Facebook account, your friend is friends with you and you are friends with your friend. It is a bidirectional relationship. So in a graph where all of the edges are bidirectional, you'll often see it like this without arrows. And when you see it like this, it is assumed that it goes both ways. So let's compare this to something like Instagram or Twitter when you follow a celebrity.

You are following the celebrity, they don't follow you back. That is directional. So the edges can be weighted or non weighted. They can be directional or bi directional. Also we have seen a graph in this course. It was a tree. Trees are a form of graphs, but they have the limitation that each node can only point to two other nodes. And when we looked at trees, we had mentioned that we had seen a tree before, which was a linked list. So linked lists are a form of a tree, and a tree is a form of a graph.

Therefore, a linked list is a form of a graph with the limitation that they can only point to one other node. But we typically think of something that looks like this when we think of graphs.

LECTURE 14.2: Graph: Adjacency Matrix
----------------------------------------

So there are two ways we're going to look at being able to represent a graph. The first one is going to be an adjacency matrix. The other one will be an adjacency list. So let's bring up a graph. And we're going to represent this in an adjacency matrix. So we'll start by looking at this a vertex. It has two edges with e and b. So we'll say a has edges with b and e and we'll just put ones in those boxes there. And of course a cannot have an edge with itself.

So we'll put a zero in that one. And then it does not have an edge with C and D. So we'll put zeros in there. Now let's look at B. It has edges with a and C. So b. Has edges with A and C, and the rest will be zeros. I'll just finish building this out like this to complete our matrix. So this axis represents the actual vertex. This axis is the items it has an edge with. So now one of the things that's interesting about an adjacency matrix, we had mentioned that A can't point to a well, also B can't point to B and so on.

You're always going to have this 45 degree line of zeros. And if you have a bidirectional matrix like we have here, you will always have a mirror image on each side of this 45 degree line that looks like that. But this is only if these are bidirectional. So let's change one of these two directional and take a look at that. We have a connection between A and B, so when the matrix that is A connects to B, that's one B connects back to A that has a one.

But if we remove the edge that points from B to A and we change this to a zero. Now we have lost our symmetry across that 45 degree line. So let's change the edge on the graph back to being bidirectional. And then we'll change it in the matrix. And now it is symmetrical. So another thing that you can have with your edges is that they can be weighted. So if they're weighted in an adjacency matrix we will just store these weights in the matrix like this instead of having once.

Okay, let's put that back. And that is our quick overview of an adjacency matrix.

LECTURE 14.4: Graph: Big O
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to look at the big O of graphs. I'm going to bring up the graph that we've been using. And we've looked at two ways of representing this graph an adjacency matrix and an adjacency list. And we're going to look at the big O of both. So first we'll look at space complexity. So if we have a vertex like a with an adjacency list we're going to store that vertex. And then we're going to store each one of the edges in an adjacency matrix.

We store those edges. But we also have to store everything that it is not connected to all of these zeros. And that is a big advantage of the adjacency list. So for space complexity the adjacency matrix is O of the number of vertices squared in the adjacency list, it's the number of vertices plus the number of edges. So from a space complexity perspective, the adjacency list is better. So let's bring back our graph. And let's say we're going to add a vertex.

We're not adding an edge yet. We'll look at that in a moment. We're just adding a vertex. And it is not connected to anything with an edge yet. So let's bring these back up and we'll look at the adjacency list first. Adding a vertex is just a matter of doing this with the adjacency matrix. We have to do this. So the adjacency matrix is going to be stored in a two dimensional array. As soon as you make those arrays bigger you have to create new arrays that are bigger.

And recreating those arrays is very inefficient. So from a big O perspective, the adjacency matrix is the number of vertices squared every time you add a vertex, whereas with an adjacency list it's O of one to add a vertex. So the adjacency list is much more efficient at doing this. So let's bring our graph back. And we're going to add an edge between B and our new vertex f like this. And let's bring these back up and look at the adjacency list first.

In order to do this we'll add an edge that goes from B to F like this. And then one that goes from f to be like this with the adjacency matrix, we just go in and change this from a zero to a one. And then we do the same thing here. And that adds that edge between f and b. So both the adjacency matrix and the adjacency list are O of one to add an edge. So let's bring back our graph and look at what it would take to remove this edge. We'll bring these back up and start with the adjacency list.

In order to remove the edge between b and f we're going to go find B. And finding a key and a hash map is O of one. But once we find B, we have to iterate through that entire array list and find f and then remove it. Then we'll come down to f and do the same thing. Here. Go through this array list. And remove be. With an adjacency matrix. We just come here and change the one to a zero. Change this one to a zero, and that edge is removed. So this is a situation where the adjacency matrix outperforms the adjacency list.

The adjacency matrix is O of one. But the adjacency list you have to go through all of the edges. And a particular vertex might have a thousand edges. And you'd have to go through the entire array list to remove the edge. So now let's bring back our graph and we will remove a vertex like that. So let's bring this back up and we'll start with the adjacency list. So when you're looking at this this looks pretty simple that you would just remove this.

But we're also going to have to come here and go through this entire array list here and see if there are any F's. Then we're going to come down here and do the same thing with this array list and so on through the entire adjacency list. So you're going to have to touch every vertex and every edge. There is a way to do this more efficiently if you have bidirectional edges. And we'll go over that when we do our method for remove vertex, because we're going to create our graph with bidirectional edges.

So now let's look at this with an adjacency matrix. In order to remove that f vertex we have to do this and rewrite the arrays. So this is also very inefficient when we remove a vertex from an adjacency matrix. So the big O of the adjacency matrix is going to be O of the number of vertices squared. The adjacency list is O of v plus e. In other words, you have to touch every vertex and every edge. So in this course we're just going to create an adjacency list.

And I want to talk a little bit more about why an adjacency list is typically better than an adjacency matrix. So we talked about with an adjacency matrix that we're not just storing the connections the ones. We also have to store all of the zeros. All of the things that we're not connected to. This becomes incredibly inefficient when you have a very large graph. So say you have something like Facebook. And we'll say for easy figuring that Facebook has a billion active users, well, you would have a graph that has instead of five like we're showing here, that would be a billion items across the top and a billion items down the left side of this graph.

Even if each of the billion users had a thousand friends, you would have literally a million zeros in that graph for every one. So that would be incredibly inefficient. So that is why in this course we are going to use an adjacency list. And that is graph big O.

LECTURE 15.1: Heap: Intro
----------------------------------------

Welcome to heaps. And I'm going to bring up this heap. And if you've never seen a heap before, the thing that's probably jumping out at you is this looks a lot like a binary search tree, and a heap is a binary tree, but the numbers are laid out in a different way. In a heap, each node has a value that is greater than each of its descendants, and because of that, the maximum value is always going to be at the top. So this node also has a value that is greater than each of its descendants.

And you can see this one does as well. A key characteristic of a heap is this tree will always be complete. And we talked about what makes a tree complete and the binary search tree section. But I'm going to review it here. So I'm going to drop these nodes out. And then I'll bring these in one by one. So a complete tree fills from left to right without any gaps. So if I add this node here it remains complete. But if we skip a spot this is no longer complete.

So I'm going to put a node into this spot to make it complete again. And then I'll add one more to finish out this line. So one of the other things that makes a heap different than a binary search tree is that in a heap you can have duplicates. So for example that 72 node could be 99 and have the same value as its parent. I'm going to go ahead and put that back as a 72. So the particular type of heap we have here is called a max heap. That's where the maximum value is at the top.

But you could also have a min heap where the minimum value is at the top. And each of the descendants is greater than. I'm going to go ahead and put this back as a max heap. So other than the maximum value being at the top, there is no particular order to the other nodes. So for example, on the second line we could take these two nodes and switch them. And this is still a valid heap. So you can do it this way. Or you could do it this way. Also in this heap we have the second and third highest values on the second line.

But that doesn't necessarily have to be the case either. We could take these two nodes and swap them and it's still a valid heap. You just need each node to be greater than each of its descendants. So I'll go ahead and put these back. So the biggest difference of all between a heap and a binary search tree in this course will be how we implement it. We implement a heap with an array list, and we're not going to have a node class. All we're going to do is store integers in this array.

So the root goes in that first spot. And then we just go line by line. So in the second line we put this value here and this value here. And then we go to the next line. And then we add all of these values as well. So there are two common ways to store the heap. The first way is to do it the way we've done it here and use the index of zero. Another common way to do it is to leave that spot open and move everything to the right. And because it is common to see it both ways, I'm going to talk about both in this course.

So you can think about this tree as being like this, where you can take all of these values and just drop them in like this. So I'm going to put this back as a tree. And I want to emphasize what are the points I made earlier about this needing to be a complete tree. If the tree was not complete, that would mean that you would have a gap in these numbers and they have to be a contiguous set of numbers. So I'll go ahead and put that back. So now we're going to do a little bit of math.

If you wanted to find the children of this node, the way you do it is you say that the left child is equal to two times the parent index. In this case the parent index is one one times two is two, and then the right child is going to be at two times the parent index plus one. In this case that is going to be three. So now let's do that for a couple more of these. Let's find the children for this node. This is at the index of two. Two times two is four.

Then plus one is five. So now let's do one more. We'll find the children of this. This node. This is at the index of three. Three times two is six plus one is seven. So now that we know how to calculate the left and right child, let's look at the calculation for finding the parent. Let's find the parent of these two nodes. And the way we'll do this is by taking the index and doing integer division by two. So if we take six and divide it by two we get three.

And if we take seven and divide it by two we get 3.5. But because we're doing integer division, everything to the right of the decimal place gets dropped. And this becomes three. And the node at the index of three is the parent node. So we'll go ahead and put these back. And the next video we're going to talk high level how it is that we insert a node into the heap. But for now that is our introduction. On to heaps.

In this video, we're going to have an introduction to how it is. We insert something into a heap. So I'm going to bring up this heap. And what we're going to do is insert the value of 100. We will always insert here, because the number one thing that we need to do with the heap is always keep it complete. So I'll go ahead and move this back over. And we do this by first comparing that 100 to its parent node. And because 100 is greater than 72 we will swap these two values. And then we will once again compare this to its parent. And because 100 is greater than 99 we will do this. Now this process of bubbling, this value up to the top is going to exist within a while loop.

And if it ever reaches the top of the heap, this will be one of the things that breaks this out of the while loop and lets us know that we now have a valid heap. Now let's try this again with a value of 75. We'll compare that 75 node to its parent. It is greater than. So we will swap these two. And now we will once again compare that node to its parent. But in this case the parent value is greater than. And this is the other situation that can break us out of our while loop.

LECTURE 15.5: Heap: Remove
----------------------------------------

So in this video we're going to write the remove method. I'll start out by bringing up a heap like this. And with remove there is only one item we will ever remove from the heap. And it doesn't matter if this is a min heap or a max heap, we will only remove and return that top item. And once we do that, we need to rearrange these nodes where this once again becomes a valid heap. And the first step in doing that is we're going to make the tree complete.

If you were going to make this tree complete in one move by moving one node, what node would you move? Well, the only node you can move to make this tree complete in one step would be to move the node that's to the bottom right. So now that we've satisfied the first step of making this complete, now we need to move that 65 node into the appropriate location. And this is similar to what we did with insert. With insert we inserted something on the bottom right.

And then we bubbled it up with remove. We take the node from the bottom right, put it at the top, and then we sink it down. And the way we sink it down is we take that 65 node, compare it to each one of its children. We determine which child has the highest value and swap it with that child. And then we'll compare it to its child again. But in this case 65 is greater than 60. And when this happens, that means we have a valid heap. And we'll just stop right there.

So I'm going to bring up this ArrayList that represents this heap. And there are three situations we need to code for. We need to write code for when we have two or more items in the heap. And then we need to write code for when we have one item in the heap, and then also write code for when we have an empty heap. So we'll start our remove method out like this. It returns an integer. So the first situation we're going to code for is when we have an empty heap.

So if the heap size is zero we're going to return null. Then we'll write code for. When we have one item in the heap we'll say if the heap size is equal to one. So we want to do two things here. We want to not only return that value of 80 but also remove it from the heap. So we're going to say return heap dot remove at the index of zero. So the heap dot remove is going to remove this. But that return statement is also going to return it. So now we're going to write code for when we have two or more items in the heap.

And the first thing we'll do here is we'll say max value equals heap dot. Get at the index of zero. So that takes this value of 80 at the index of zero and sets max value to be equal to 80. And we will return that at the end of this method. So technically this number 80 at the index of zero is going to remain in the array list. But we know we're going to remove that and overwrite it. So I'm going to gray this out in the array list and in the tree.

So what we want to do next in the tree is move that bottom right node up to the top like this. In the array list. What we want to do is remove the last item and then move it down here and take that value and put it in at the index of zero. And we'll do that with this line of code, which removes the last item, and then sets the index of zero to be equal to that value. So now what we need to do is take this node with the value of 60 and sink this down in the tree to its appropriate location.

And we're going to do that with the helper method that we write in the next video. So what I'm going to do here is I'm just going to say sink down. We're going to pass it the index of zero. And it's this sink down method that's going to take that 60 node and sink it down into the tree to its appropriate spot in the array list. That's going to look like this. Now the only thing left to do is to return max value, that value of 80 that was at the top of the heap before we started this process.

And that is all of the code for the remove method. So we'll write the sink down method in the next video. But for now that is our method for remove.

LECTURE 15.6: Heap: Sink Down
----------------------------------------

So in this video we're going to write the sink down method. In the last video in the remove method, the remove method would remove this node and return it, and it would move this node up to the top. But what we did not write code for was sinking this node down to the appropriate place in the heap. And that's the code we're going to write here. So sink down is going to take that 65 node and compare it to each of its children, and will determine which of the children has the highest value.

And then do a swap with that child. And then we'll compare it to its children again. But in this case, because 50 is less than 65, that's going to break us out of our loop. And then we're going to stop right here. So once again, sink down is a helper method that will be used by the remove method down here. And we're going to pass this the index of zero which is the root. So before we get into our sink down code I'm going to move that 65 back up to the top of the tree.

And then we'll start our sink down code like this. So the way we're using sink down as a helper method for the remove method, it is not necessary to pass this in index. But I'm going to do it this way because there are other common heap methods that will use sink down as a helper method as well. And those methods require sink down to have index as a parameter in the sink down method. So index is going to point to the root. It will be the index of zero.

And then we will also have max index that is set equal to index like this. And that brings us to our while loop. And we're going to say while true and the way that we will break out of the while loop is with a return statement. So just to make our code a little bit more readable, I'm going to have a couple of variables called left index and right index. And these will be the indexes of the left child and the right child in this case that 75 and 80 node.

So first we're going to compare that 65 node to its left child with this if statement. So we're going to see if the value at left index is greater than the value at max index. And in this case it is because 75 is greater than 65. So we're going to say max index equals left index which moves this down here. And you can see that those two are not pointing to the same place. So that is the case here. We're going to do a swap with index and right index which will make our tree look like this.

From the perspective of the array list. What we have done is swapped these two numbers. And then we're going to add one more line here. We're going to say index equals max index. And that will move that over like this. So let's bring back the rest of our code. And for a moment what I'm going to do is remove the if statements and focus in on these two lines of code. So these two helper methods for left child and right child do not determine if the index they're returning is a valid index in the heap.

So for example that 65 node, you can see that we have a valid left child. But on the right side there is not a node there. And if you run the right child method, it is going to return the index of six. It's just going to return that number. But it doesn't check to see if that is a valid part of the heap.

So now let's bring back all of our code again. And in this iteration of this while loop we're going to change. All three of these if statements, all three of these are going to have code added to them. So I'm going to move these if statements up here. So I'm going to bring up the array list again. And right index would be at the index of six. So the size of this array list is six.

LECTURE 15.7: Heap: Priority Queues & Big O
----------------------------------------

So now let's talk about priority queues. And priority queues are most often implemented with a heap, so much so that oftentimes you'll hear people use the terms priority queue and heap interchangeably. So with a priority queue, what you want to do is be able to return the highest priority item in the queue. And you can just intuitively tell that with a heap. A heap is going to be a great data structure for doing that. And the reason why it is almost always used for implementing a priority queue just comes down to big O, but technically you could implement a priority queue with something like a linked list, but returning the maximum value here requires that you iterate through the entire linked list to determine the maximum value, and then return it, and that's going to be O of N.

You could also implement a priority queue with something like an array list. This array list is not set up as a heap. These numbers are just in a random order. So to find the maximum value would require that we iterate through the entire array list. Once again, this is o of n, so you might look at that and say, well, how about using a hash map. Because going to this maximum value would be o of one. The problem with that is you don't know that 95 is the maximum value.

We would have to iterate through all of these to determine that that is the maximum value. Once again, this is O of n. Another data structure that we could use would be a binary search tree. So this is not a heap here. This is set up as a binary search tree. And if the tree is balanced getting to the maximum value, you can just go all the way to the right and that would be log n. Also adding a value. Let's say we add the value of 100. We would iterate through the tree and place that to the right of the 95 node.

That is also log in, but that's only the case if this tree is balanced. But with a binary search tree, it is possible to have something that looks like this. Now, getting to that maximum value requires that we iterate through every item which is O of n. Also, if we added the number 100 once again, we would have to iterate through all of these to place that to the right of the 95. So now let's compare this to using a heap. So one of the things that makes a heap very efficient for a priority queue is that it is always balanced, because the tree is always complete.

Now to understand the big O of a heap, you have to be able to understand how we calculate it. The height of the heap. And we talked about this on the binary search tree section. But the height of the tree is going to be log n. So this tree here is seven nodes. But we'll say this is approximately eight nodes. And the height is three. And log sub two of eight is three. So now that we know that the height is log in, let's look at what happens when we remove an item.

This node gets moved to the top and the farthest that we'll ever have to move. That 50 node is sinking it down the entire height of the tree. And the height is log n. So now let's look at adding a node. We'll add the value of 100. And when we bubble this up the farthest that we'll have to bubble the 100 up is bubbling it up the entire height of the tree. So it doesn't matter if we're doing insert or remove of it is O of log n and O of log n is incredibly efficient.

So if we had a heap with a million items in it, n would be a million and log n would be about 20. So it's a huge difference in efficiency. And that's why you will almost always see a heap being used with priority queues.

LECTURE 16.1: Recursion: Intro
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to talk about recursion. So my favorite definition of recursion is that it's a method that calls itself until it doesn't. So to explain this I'm going to bring up a gift box. And we need to open this gift box. And the way to do that of course is with the open gift box method. And when we open this gift box there are two possibilities. Either the gift box contains a ball or it contains a new smaller gift box. So how are we going to open this gift box?

Well, we're going to do it with the open gift box method. And you can see that we're using the same method for both boxes. And once again we could get a ball or a new smaller gift box. And then we'll use the same method to open this one. And let's say this one contains the ball. Well obviously we're not going to run the open gift box method on this. Which brings us back to that definition at the beginning, that it's a method that calls itself until it doesn't.

Well, it doesn't call itself once we get to the ball. So let's put these boxes and the ball back and we're going to write some pseudo code. So I want to emphasize that this code I'll try to make it look like Java code. But this is not real code. This is for example purposes only in case we're going to do this. This is going to be our open gift box method. And the return type is ball. Which means once we open a box and it contains a ball we will return it.

So when we open a gift box, if we'll say is ball, it contains a ball, we will return the ball. Now technically we would need to have some kind of variable that is of type ball. And we're going to create a new ball. And I'm not going to put that in here. I'm just going to simplify this and take this out. But suffice it to say we are dealing with some object that we're calling ball. But if we open a box and it does not contain a ball, we want to run the open gift box method.

Again, this is where the method calls itself. So a couple of things about our recursive method or any recursive method. The process of opening each new box is the same, or the process of doing whatever you're doing with recursion. Each time you run that recursive method, it is doing the same thing. The other thing is that each time we open a box, we make the problem smaller. So each of these boxes got smaller and smaller until we got to the ball.

So any recursive method, each time you call it it is working on a smaller problem. So let's remove these and walk through this code. Since we have a gift box, we need to run the open gift box method. We'll check to see if it is a ball that it contains. In this case we're going to say it's not. It's a gift box. And that will cause this line to run, which will initiate a new instance of the open gift box method. And then we'll check to see if this box contains a ball.

It does not. It's a gift box which causes this line to run, which creates another instance of the open gift box method. This time though, this if statement is ball, this time it is a ball, so we'll just return the ball. And because it's a return statement, we stop running code and we do not move to this line. So it's essential to have some type of return statement here, to break us out of the loop of having the method call itself over and over. So let's put these boxes back like this and talk a little bit of terminology.

When you have a situation where you get to the ball or whatever it is that causes you to stop having the method call itself. This is called a base case. So if you have a situation like this where the method is going to call itself again because you have another gift box, this is called a recursive case. And this would be a recursive case and this would be a base case. And when you're using recursion you always have to have a base case. So let's say we have a situation like this.

And instead of having an if statement we remove it like this. So what happens when you don't have a base case is when you run the open gift box method. You will then run this line which initiates another instance of the open gift box method, which will cause this line to run. And then you'll have another instance of the open gift box method and so on, and you'll get what is called a stack Overflow. So you have to have a base case. But there are a couple of ways that you could have an if statement here and still not have a base case, because this if statement has to be true at some point.

So let's say you have something like this. If one is greater than two. Well one is never greater than two obviously. So this is never going to be true. This is essentially not having a base case. And you might look at that and say, well obviously one is never going to be greater than two. But sometimes the conditional you have in an if statement can be really complex. So if you get a stack overflow, this will be one of the places to go back and troubleshoot your code.

So let's put this back. So the other thing that you need to have is a return statement, because that causes the code to stop running. Let's say we remove this return statement and instead we just print hello out to the screen. So what happens in this situation where we finally get to the point where is ball is true in the if statement. It's just going to print out hello. Then we're going to go down to here and we'll add another open gift box method and continue that infinite loop of adding instances of the open gift box method to the stack, which is going to cause a stack overflow.

So the other thing that's essential here is we have to have a return statement. And that's our overview of recursion.

LECTURE 16.2: Call Stack
----------------------------------------

So to understand recursion, it's important to understand the concept of the call stack, because every time we call something recursively, we're putting a new method on the call stack. And earlier in the course we actually created a data structure called a stack. And we said it was like a can of tennis balls. Well this analogy applies to a call stack as well. So if you have a method that is running, that's the method at the top of the call stack.

That's that top tennis ball. When it gets done running, it is only then that the next method can start running again until it finishes. And that's popped from the call stack. And then finally, that last method on the call stack can run and we remove that. So let's move this over to the side. And we're going to use this for our call stack. Let's say we call a method that's called method one, and all it does is print out a string that says one. What do we call this method?

It gets added to the call stack like this. Then we print out one. And then this method is done running and we pop it from the call stack. But let's bring this back and put it on the call stack and add a line to our method. We're going to have this method call method two. And when it calls method two it puts method two on the call stack. And all this is going to do is print out a string that says two. But before we print that out, we're going to have this method call method three, which will push method three onto the call stack.

And this method is going to print out a string that says three. Except we won't have this one call another method. Since method three is at the top of the call stack, it's the only method that can run right now. So we'll print out this string three. And now method three is done running. And we can pop that from the call stack. So now method two is the active method. Running. Method two was waiting for method three to be done and be popped from the call stack.

So it could move to this line of code and print out two. And that was the last thing that method two is going to do. So we can pop that from the call stack. So now you can see that method one is at the top of the call stack. That means method one is the method that is actively running. Method one was waiting for method two to finish running so it could go to this line. Print out one. And now we can pop that from the call stack. So notice that the order that we called our methods was one, two, three, but it printed out three, two, one.

And the reason for that is that's the order the methods came off of the call stack. So let's bring our three methods up like this. And then we'll look at all three of these and IntelliJ. There are the three methods that we just created there. And then of course, we have to have a main method that's down here. And the only thing the main method does is kick things off by calling method one. So instead of just running this, I'm going to do something a little different.

Right here next to method one I'm going to come over here next to this line number. And I'm going to click. And we get a red circle. And you can see this whole line is highlighted and red. Then I'm going to come up here to this button that says debug. If I hover over this it says debug main and I will click on this. So this has started running our code. And you can see on the call stack here we have main that is this main method. Then down here you can see this is highlighted.

The highlighted line is the line that is about to run. So the really important button here is this one right here. So I'm going to hover my mouse pointer here and it says step into. And clicking this button over and over will allow us to walk through all of this code line by line. So like I said, this highlighted line is the one about to run if I click this. You can see this has added method one to our call stack. And the highlighted line here shows that we're about to launch method two.

So if I click this again you can see this has added method two to our call stack. Our highlighted line here shows we're about to start method three. So I'll click on this again. And you can see that method three has been added to the call stack. So this line that's about to run here is going to print out the string three. So I'll click on this. And you can see this console tab here has this little yellow marker here. I'm going to click on the console.

And you can see it's printed out three. So I'm going to click back on the button on the left for the debugger. And keep walking through this. So this line here is showing that this method three is done running. And we're about to pop this from the call stack. So when I click the step into. You can see that removed method three from the call stack. Now we're about to print out two. I click the step into like this. And our console. Now says two. So I'll move back over here to the debugger and do the step into again.

And because this is at the end of method two where it's highlighted, that means that's going to pop method two off the call stack. And now method one, the only thing left for it to do is to print out one. I'll click on this again. And move over to this console, and you can see that that's where it's printed out, one to the console. And I'll move back to the debugger here. And that was the last thing that method one needed to do. So when I step into this again, it's going to pop method one off the call stack.

And now you can see down here that we're at that closing bracket for the main method, which means it's going to pop main off the call stack. Like that. And that runs all of our code. So like I said at the beginning of the video, to understand recursion, it's important to understand the call stack that each time the method is calling itself, it's creating a new instance of a method and adding that to the call stack. So we'll use an example where we're walking through the call stack with a recursive method in the next video.

But for now that is our overview of the call stack.

LECTURE 17.1: rBST: Contains
----------------------------------------

So in this section we're going to revisit binary search trees. And we're going to start out by redoing the contains method. We already did this previously in the previous binary search tree section. But when we did this there we did it iteratively, which is to say that we used a loop. And now we're going to write it recursively. So we'll start our code out like this. And we'll call it our contains for recursive contains. But notice that this is private.

So an end user is not going to call this method directly. And the reason for that is we need to kick this off by passing this the root node. And then of course we'll pass it the value that we're checking to see if the binary search tree contains that value. But the method that an end user will use to call this will be this method here. And notice that it has the same name. This is called method overloading, and Java will know which one you're calling based on what arguments that you pass it.

If you call our contains with only an integer, Java will know that you're calling this second method, and when you call this, it will pass that value down to here. And then it will call our contains with the root and the value. Now the first situation that we're going to code for is when we have an empty tree. Because obviously the tree is not going to contain the value that you're looking for if the tree is empty. So we're going to say if current node is equal to null.

And remember that current node in this case is the root, then we're going to return false. But let's say that we're looking for the number 47. And root is pointing to the 47 node. In this case we'll say if current node value is equal to the value that we're looking for we'll return true. Now where this becomes more complicated is if you're looking for a value that is further down in the tree. So let's say we're looking for the number 21. If this is the case, we have to traverse through the tree to get to that node.

So I'm going to bring up a call stack on the right. And we'll kick this off by calling an instance on the root. And then I also have that 21 there because that's the value that we're looking for. So now we'll say if the value that we're looking for is less than current node dot value. And that is the case here, we're going to return our contains. That's where this is recursive. And then we'll call this on current node dot left. And we'll also pass this instance that value of 21.

And then we'll add this to the call stack. So now this is the active instance on the call stack. And we start from the top. We'll say if current node is equal to null. And that is not the case here. So we move down to here and say if current node dot value equals value. And that is the case here, we're going to return true. And when it returns it it's going to return it to the calling method. And once it does that this is popped from the call stack.

So now this is the active instance. Again the last line that it ran was this one. And this portion of this line is what called that method that we just popped from the call stack. So when it returned true, it's specifically returning it to here. And this has a return statement as well. And the place is going to return it to is the calling method. And specifically it's going to return it to this portion here. And when it returns that this gets popped from the call stack.

But this has a return statement as well. And this is going to return this to the original method call. It's going to return it to here. So this is how you have true returned all the way back to where you called this. So now let's look at a situation where we're looking for a value that is not in the tree. So once again we're going to look for the 21 node. And I'll add this to the call stack. And this if statement is what applies. 21 is less than current node dot value.

But in this case current node dot left is equal to null. So. Are going to call an instance on null. And now this is the active instance on the call stack. And we start at the top and say if current node is equal to null. And that is the case here, we're going to return false and we'll return false to the calling method. And this gets popped from the call stack. Now this is the active instance on the call stack. This is where it left off. And false is returned to here.

So when this method returns false it will return it to the calling method which returns it to here. And that will cause this to be popped from the call stack. ----------------------------------------

So in this video, we're going to look high level at what is required to delete a node from a binary search tree. And there are two parts to this. First, if we're going to delete that 27 node, we have to start at the root and then traverse down to the 21 node and then finally get down to the 27 node. So the first part of this is we have to be able to traverse through the tree. And then the second part is once we find this node we have to be able to delete it.

Now in this situation we are deleting a leaf node. And that's our simplest scenario. But what if we have nodes on the left. How do we delete the 27 node then. Well this is actually easier than you might think. We just need to move this up. And this is a valid binary search tree. So now let's look at the situation. When we have a subtree on the right we can actually do the same thing here and move this subtree up. And this is once again a valid binary search tree.

Where it gets more complicated is if we have a subtree on each side, we can't just move one of these subtrees up. So what we're going to do here is we're going to look at the subtree on the right of the node that we want to delete. And we'll find the node with the lowest value. That's going to be the 28 node. And we're going to copy that value of 28 into this node up here. Now let me be clear. We're not moving that 28 node up. We're just copying the value into the existing node.

And at the moment that we do that we have 228 nodes in the binary search tree. So then what we're going to do is remove that second 28 node. And now this is a valid binary search tree. In the next few videos we will walk through all of these scenarios. But for now that is our introduction to delete node.

SECTION 18: Tree Traversal

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 18.1: Tree Traversal: Intro
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to do tree traversal. And what tree traversal is, is that we're going to visit every node in the tree. And then we'll put the values in an array list. And then we will return that array list. But tree traversal is a little more complicated than doing something like a linked list, because with a linked list, because it's just linear. If we're going to traverse this, we just start at the beginning and then we just go through the list.

But with a tree, there were multiple ways to visit each node. We could do something like this where we start at the top, and then we do the next row like this, and then we do the next row like this, and so on. This is called breadth first search. Another way that we could do this is we could start all the way to the bottom left and then come up and then come down, and then come up to the first item and then go all the way to the bottom left of the items remaining like this, and then come up and then come down.

And this is an example of what is called depth. First search. And we're going to look at three different ways of doing depth. First search. But for now, that is our quick overview of tree traversal.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 18.2: BFS (Breadth First Search): Intro
----------------------------------------

So in this video, we're going to walk through the steps for breadth first search. And then in the next video we'll write the code. So I'm going to bring up a tree like this. And in breadth first search this is the one where we start at the top. And then we do the next row. And we do this row by row and add these numbers to an array list. So the way we're going to do this, I'm going to move this over to the side. First we're going to have a Q, and then we're also going to have an array list called results.

So in the Q we're going to store nodes. And obviously this is going to be a pointer to a node. But we'll have access to all of the attributes to this node. So I'm going to change this node to look like this I'll keep it as blue since that's the color of our nodes. But it's important to understand that we're not just storing the value. And we will be using the value and the left and the right from that node. So what we're going to do with that node that's in the queue is we will dequeue it and store the value in results.

And I change that color to green because we're only storing the value, not the entire node. And then from that 47 node in the tree, we're going to go left and to the left of the 47 node is the 21 node. We'll put that in the queue. And to the right is the 76 node. And we'll put that in the queue. Then we will dequeue the 21 put its value in results. And from the 21 we're going to go left and add the 18 and then right and add the 27 to the queue. And once we've gone left and right we will dequeue the next item which is 76.

And then from the 76 node we're going to go left. And that is the 52. And right. That is the 82. And now that we've gone left and right we'll dequeue the 18. But the 18 doesn't have anything on the left or the right. So we can just go to the 27. Same thing with the 27. It doesn't have anything on the left or the right. And that is true of the 52. And the 82. And we're going to have a loop that runs until that queue is empty. And when the queue is empty, we know that we have all of the values added to results.

So I'll remove the queue. So now I want to just look at the numbers here and show you the pattern that they're in. If you take this 47 and put it at the top and take the next two and put them here, you can see that this exactly matches our tree. So we will code this in the next video. But for now this is our introduction to breadth first search.

So now we can bring in the while loop, and this while loop will run until the queue is empty. I'm going to move this up and focus on this while loop now. Like this. Then we'll say if there's a node on the right, we'll add that node to the queue like this. So now we'll run through the while loop again. So these two steps right here I'm going to do these in one step moving forward where we remove the item from the queue and then add it to results. I'll just have that number come straight down from now on. And then we'll check to see if there's an item on the left of the 21 node. There is. And then we'll check to see if there's one on the right.

And there is. And those both get added to the queue. And then we come back up to the top of the while loop. And we'll remove this item from the queue, move that to results, and then we'll add the node to the left of the 76 and the right of the 76. And now we're going to remove that 18 from the queue. Now the 18 doesn't have anything on the left or the right because it's a leaf node. And the same is going to be true of the next three nodes the 27, the 52 and the 82.

And now our queue is empty and that breaks us out of our while loop. So I'll remove the queue from the bottom there. And now let's bring back the rest of our code. The only thing left to do is to return that results array list. So that is all of our code for the breadth first search method. Remember this is going to be inside of the binary search tree class. And we'll look at this in a moment in IntelliJ. And when we do we'll build this tree. And then we'll expect to get this array list returned.

So now let's flip over to IntelliJ and take a look at this. So there is our breadth first search method that we just built added to our binary search tree class. Then I'll come up here to our main class that contains our main method. And with this line here, we'll create a new binary search tree called my binary search tree. And with these lines, we'll build the tree that we just saw. And then down here we're going to run breadth first search and print out the array list that it returns.

And I'll run this. And this is returned the array list that we expected. And that is our method for breadth first search.

But it is a leaf node so it doesn't have anything to the left or the right. So we can pop that from the call stack. The 76 has now gone left and right. And we can pop that from the call stack. And the 47 has also gone left and right now. And we can pop that from the call stack. So now we're done running all of our instances of traverse. Now let's look at depth. First search post order. I'm going to bring up a tree here. In this video we'll just walk through the steps of it and then we'll code it in the next video. And just like with all the other tree traversals, we're going to start at the top. What's different here is that we're just going to visit that 47 node. We're not going to write that value to the results list yet. Then we're going to go to the left and then visit the 21, and we'll go to the left again to the 18.

Now, what the 18 is going to do is it's going to look to the left. There's no node there. It'll look to the right and there is no node there. And then finally it will write its value to the results list. So the order that we're doing this is we look left, we look right. And then we write the value from that node. So now we come up to the 21. It has gone left. Now it's going to go right to the 27. It's going to look to the left. It's going to look to the right.

And then finally we will write the value 27 to the results list. Now we come back up to the 21 again. Now it has gone left. It has gone right now we can write that value to the list. And that brings us back up to the 47. It has gone left now it will go right to the 76 node. And we always start with going left first. So it will go to the 52 node. It will look left and right and then write its value. We come back up to the 76. It has gone left. Now it will go write that 82 node will look left and then right, and then write its value.

That brings us back up to the 76. It has now gone left and right and it can write its value. And that brings us back up to the 47. It has gone left. It has gone right. And now its value can be written to the results list. So we'll code this in the next video. But that is our overview of depth. First search post order.
