----------------------------------------

Okay. So now let's look at depth. First search in order. We'll bring up our tree like this. And what we're going to do is we're going to visit that first node. We're going to go to the left. Go to the left again the 18 will try to go to the left. There's nothing there. We'll write that 18 to our results list and then it'll go to the right. So at this point the tree looks exactly the same as it did at this point with depth. First search postorder.

But instead of going left, then right, and then writing its value, it's going left, writing its value, and then going right. So we're going to get this in a different order. So now we're going to come up to the 21. It has gone left. Now it's going to write its value and then go write. The 27 will go left write its value and then go right. That brings us up to the 47 node. It has gone left. Now we can write its value and go write. The 76 will start out by going left.

The 52 will go left, write its value and then go right. The 76 has already gone left. Now we can write its value to the results list and then go right. Then the 82 will go left and then write its value, and then try to go right. So at this point, what I'm going to do is turn the entire tree back to blue and look just at the order that the items get written to the list. We start out with the 18 being written to the list, and this is the lowest value in the tree.

Then it's the 21, the next lowest, and so on. Then the 47, the 52 and the 76 and the 82. So all of the numbers are written to the list in numerical order. So that is depth first search in order.

LECTURE 19.1: Bubble Sort: Intro
----------------------------------------

All right. Now we're going to look at our first sorting algorithm bubble sort. And in this video we'll walk through the logic of how bubble sort works. And in the next video we'll write the code. So we're going to sort. This list, except I'm going to change it to look like this so we can visually see the size of the numbers. And previously I had shown lists to be in green. And what I'm going to do here is I'm only going to show these to be in green once they're sorted.

So I'm going to move this back. And with bubble sort, what we're going to do is we're going to start with the first item in the list, and we're going to compare it to the second item. And if the first item is larger than the second item. We're going to switch these. Then we'll take the second item and compare it to the third item. And in this case these don't need to be switched. So we'll go to the third item and compare it to the fourth item. These do need to be switched, and the fourth item with the fifth item, and the fifth item with the sixth item.

And now that six is sorted, what we've done is we've bubbled up the largest item. So in order to do this, we had to do five comparisons. We had six items and we did five comparisons. Now we only have five items left to sort. So in this round we'll only have four comparisons. So we'll start back at the beginning and compare the first item to the second item that doesn't need to be switched, the second item to the third item, the third to the fourth, the fourth to the fifth.

That's four comparisons. And now the second largest item out of the list has been sorted. It has been bubbled up. So let's just finish sorting these. We'll compare first to the second, second to the third, third to the fourth. Now that item is sorted. And they will compare first to second, second to third, and now the third item is sorted. And then we'll just compare these two. And if they need to be switched, they'll be switched. Otherwise we know they are both sorted.

And now we have a completely sorted list. So now I'm going to put this back the way it was. And this is where we're going to start in our next video when we code. Bubblesort.

LECTURE 19.2: Bubble Sort: Code
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to code bubble sort. We'll pick up where we left off in the last video, except I'll take this and shrink it down into the corner to make room for our code. So we'll call this bubble sort and we'll pass this an array of integers. So one of the things we talked about in the last video is our first pass through this array that has six items. There will be five comparisons. And then the second time there's four and then three and so on.

So in our first for loop, we're going to say that I is equal to the length of the array minus one. So the length is six. So the first time we're going to go through five times. And then you can see here that we are decrementing this each time. In the second for loop. We're going to run this I times. So that'll be five times. The first time we do it and then four and three, etc.. So inside of this for loop is where we will check to see if two items need to be switched.

So we'll say array at the index of J. That's this item here. If it's greater than j plus one which is the next item, then we'll switch these. So we'll switch these with three lines of code. I'm just going to bring them in. And then we'll walk through how this works. So we're creating a new integer temp. And we're setting it equal to the value of the item that's at the index of J. So to show this graphically temp equals this value here. Then we'll set the item at the index of J to be equal to the value of j plus one.

So that's going to do this. This moves that value over there. And then we say at the index of J plus one we're going to set that equal to temp. And that copies that in there. And that's how we're going to swap our items. So if we keep running these for loops it'll cause the entire array to look like this. It will sort the entire array. So notice that we don't have a return statement. So what we're doing with this type of sorting algorithm is we are sorting the existing array in place.

We're not making a copy and then sorting it and then returning it. We are actually sorting the original array in place without making a copy of it. So that's why the return type is void. And this will be the case for most of our sorting algorithms, but not all. When we get to merge sort, merge sort is actually making copies and then putting them together and then returning a copy of the array. But because we don't have to make a copy of the array and we are sorting it in place.

The space complexity of bubble sort is O of one, which basically means we don't have to make a copy. So this is all of our code for bubble sort. We'll look at this code in a moment in IntelliJ. And when we do we'll start with an array that looks like this. And we'll run bubble sort on that array and sort it. So now let's flip over and take a look at this. And then with this line here we'll run bubble sort on my array. And then down here we're going to print that array out and show that it's sorted. So in this video we're going to walk through the steps for selection sort. And then in the next video we will code this. So I'll start out by bringing up our array that will represent like this. But with selection sort we're also going to need the indexes. So I'll go ahead and bring those up. So with selection sort we start with this first item. And what we're going to do is we're going to look for the index that contains the lowest value. So this is the only one we've looked at so far.

So we're going to have a variable called min index. And we're going to set it to zero. So I want to emphasize that this variable does not hold the value of four. It holds the index of zero. So then what we'll have is a loop where we start with the next item and say is the value of this item less than the one at the index of zero. And in this case it is. So we're going to set min index to be equal to one. Then we'll look at the next item. This one is not less than.

So we won't do anything there or there. But when we get to this value this one is lower. So we'll update min index to be equal to four. Then we'll continue through the end of the array. And once we've finished looping through, we know that our min index is at the index of four. So now what we're going to do is take these two items and swap them. And when we do that one is sorted. So I'll color that in green. And then we'll start the process again with the next item.

We'll set min index equal to one. And then we'll have a loop that loops through the rest of the array. And you can tell by looking at this that the two is the lowest item. So this is a different situation. We're not going to have a swap. And that's something we'll talk about when we code this. How do we handle the situation when we don't need to do a swap. But we are going to loop through this like this. And when we get to the end min index is equal to one.

And that means that item is sorted. So let's do this for the six and min index is two. And I'm going to move this variable over because we're going to run out of room over there in a moment. But we compare it to the five. Now that's the lowest then the four that's the lowest and the three. And now that item is the lowest. And now that we've run to the end, we know that the min index is that the index of five. And we'll swap these. And now that three is sorted.

So now we just have a couple more times to run through this. So we'll just take the five min index. Is that the index of three. That next item is lower. So we'll update min index. And now we know min index is at the index of four. We swap these. The four is sorted. And we'll do this one more time where we compare the five at the index of four to the six. And these don't need to be swapped. So these are both sorted. So we'll code this in the next video.

And when we do we'll kick off that video with an array that looks like this. And then we'll write the code to sort that. But for now that is our overview of selection sort.

LECTURE 19.4: Selection Sort: Code
----------------------------------------

So now we're going to code selection sort. We'll pick up where we left off in the last video with this array. And we'll shrink that down into the corner like that. And then we'll jump right into writing the code. So we're going to call this selection sort. And we'll pass it an array that we're just going to call array. And the first thing we're going to do is create a for loop that starts at the index of zero and goes through the length of the array.

So let's take a look at that variable I from the for loop. That's going to be this item here. And this is what we initialize min index with that index of zero. So min index needs to be equal to I. So let's add this in with the rest of our code min index is going to be an int. And we'll set it equal to I from the for loop. And once we do that we'll create another for loop that compares that to the rest of the items in the array. So notice that we start this at I plus one.

So our first time through this this index is going to be I. And then J will be I plus one. So we're going to start out with min index being equal to I, which in this case is the index of zero. ----------------------------------------
LECTURE 20.1: Merge Sort: Overview
----------------------------------------

Now let's do merge sort. So the idea behind merge sort is this if you have two sorted lists and these are both sorted, it is very easy to combine these into a new sorted list. So let's say we started with a list with eight items unsorted like this. What merge sort is going to do is first, break this list in half and break it in half again and break it in half again, until the only thing that we have left are lists that only have one item in them.

So there will be eight separate lists with one item in each list, and a list with one item in. It is by definition sorted. So now that you've gotten these all down to single item lists, we can take two of the items. And create a new list that's sorted, and we'll do that with the next two. And so on until we have four lists, in this case with two items each. Then we'll do it again with these two lists like that. And with those two lists. And now we can take these two lists and combine them into one sorted list.

And that is our high level overview of merge sort.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 20.2: Merge: Intro
----------------------------------------

So in this video we're going to talk about our merge function. Merge is what is called a helper function. It doesn't do all of what is required for merge sort. It just does a portion of it. So the portion that merge does is it takes two sorted lists. And they do need to be sorted lists. And it takes the values from these and puts them into a new combined list like this. So let's put this back like this and walk through how we would do that. So we're going to loop through each of these lists.

And we'll loop through the first one with a variable I and the second one with a variable j. Then what we'll do is just compare I and j. Whichever one is lowest, we will add to the new combined list. Then we'll do it again. In this case j is lower so we'll add that one. Then we just keep going through making these comparisons. And we do this until one of the lists is empty. Once we've done that, we'll have another loop that loops through whichever list still has items.

And we'll add these items to the combined list. So we will code this helper function in the next video. But for now that is our introduction. To merge.

LECTURE 20.4: Merge Sort: Intro
----------------------------------------

So in the last video we created merge that takes two sorted lists and puts them together. Now we're going to look at Merge Sort. That also needs to take a list and break it in half, and then break it in half again and then do it again. Once we've gone through this process of breaking it in half, it is only then that we can use that helper function merge to take sorted lists and combine them like this. And then we'll do it again and do it again. To create the sorted list.

So what we're going to do here is write the code that breaks lists in half. And this is going to be the first time that we've used recursion. Remember we had two rules for being able to use recursion. First, it has to do the same thing over and over. And this is just going to break lists in half over and over. So it meets that criteria. The second criteria is that you have to be making the problem smaller. Well if you're breaking things in half then by definition you're making things smaller.

So this is a perfect place to use recursion. And we're going to use the recursion until we reach a base case. And the base case is going to be when we've broken it down to where the length of the list is one. So once we've done our recursion to break things in half, we've reached our base case to stop breaking things in half. When we've gotten down to one item. That is when we'll use merge, which we created in the last video to put the list back together.

So these will be the three steps of merge sort. Break it in half until we get it down to one. Then we use merge to put everything back together. And that is our quick overview. Of Mergesort.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 20.6: Merge Sort: Big O
----------------------------------------

Now let's look at the big O of merge sort. Let's bring up a list here. And the first thing I'm going to talk about is space complexity. So with Merge Sort you start out with this original list. Then you create two new lists called left and right. And then you continue breaking these down. So you start out with your original list. And then you have eight new lists that each have a length of one. So the number of items being stored in memory have been doubled.

So that means this is O of N for space complexity. The previous sorting algorithms that we've looked at are sorted in place. That means we take the original list and we move things around inside of that list. Merge sort is different than that in that it creates new lists. So let's put this back up here and look at time complexity. When we're breaking this in half, we do this in one, two, three steps. We had eight items. It took us three steps. This is going to be log in because two to the third power is eight.

It took us three steps to break this in half with eight items. But let's look at what happens when we put these together like this. So think back to our merge function. Our helper function. It had those while loops in there that went through each number one by one to be able to combine these into a sorted list. So breaking these apart is O of log n, putting it back together because of having to loop through each item with the merge function is o of n, and so that's where we get the o of n times log n time complexity for merge sort.

So let's look at this on the graph. And when it comes to sorting algorithms, only the two on the left really matter. I'm going to gray out the ones on the right. The first three sorting algorithms that we created were O of n squared, bubble sort, insertion sort, selection sort. But this one is much more efficient O of n times log n o of n times. Log n is the most efficient that you can make a sorting algorithm that's going to sort multiple types of data.

There are certain efficiencies and sorting only numbers, and there are some sorting algorithms that can run more efficient than this. But you can only sort numbers with them. But if you're going to sort multiple types of data, this is as good as it gets. So o of n times log n is much more efficient than O of n squared. And it may not look that way on this graph, but the axis on the left, the y axis only goes up to 75. If the axis went to a thousand or it went to a million, you would see that there is a huge difference between O of n squared and O of n times log n, and that is our overview of merge sort.

Big O.

The six is greater than and I'll color that in gray. The one is less than. I'll color that in yellow.

And we'll swap that with the seven, because the seven is now the first item in the list that is greater than the four.

Like this. And then the five is greater than. So we have all of the items that are less than the four together, and all of the items that are greater than the four together. And then what we're going to do is run quicksort again on these items as well as on these items. We'll start on the left. First two will be our pivot. In this case one is less. The three is greater than we swap these two items. And now the two is sorted. Then we'll run quicksort on these two. And because there's only one item in each of these, we know those are sorted. Then we'll run quicksort. On this side the pivot is going to be six. Seven is greater five is going to be less.

We're going to swap these. And they will swap these. The six is sorted. We'll run quicksort on these. And because there's only one item in each of these we know these are sorted. And now we have a completely sorted list. And that is our quick overview of quicksort.

LECTURE 21.2: Pivot: Intro
----------------------------------------

So in this video, we're going to walk through how our pivot function is going to work. And pivot is going to be a helper function that we use for quicksort. So this is similar to how we use the merge function as a helper function in merge sort. So to explain it I'm going to bring up a list. Except we'll show it like this. And what we're going to do in pivot is we're going to pick a pivot point. That's going to be the first item. Then we're going to have all the items that are less than the pivot value on one side, and all the ones that are greater on the other side.

And then we'll swap these. And this makes it where that for value is in the spot where it will be when the list is sorted. So pivot is going to do two things. It's going to get the list to this point where it looks like this. But it's also going to return the index of that for value. And we'll talk about why we're going to return the index instead of the value in a moment. But for now I'm going to put this back like this. And we'll walk through how we're going to make this work.

So we're going to have a pivot value. And then we're going to compare it to everything after that with a for loop. So the for loop is going to start here. We're going to call the variable in the for loop I we're also going to have a variable that points at this pivot point. The variable is actually going to be called pivot index. But I'm just going to say pivot on the arrow here to make it fit. And we'll have another variable called swap index. I'll just call it swap on the arrow here also to make that fit.

And then we'll start running through the for loop. So the first value for I is greater than the pivot value. So we'll just color that in gray. And then I moves to the next index. Now this one is less than. So what we're going to do is move swap over one, and then we'll swap the values at swap index. And I like this. Then I will move forward again. This one's greater than this one is less than the pivot index value. So we'll move swap up one and then we'll swap these two values.

This is also less so. We'll move. Swap up. Exchange these two, and then we get to the end of the for loop and we're done with the I variable. So then what we're going to do is swap the items at the pivot index and the swap index like this. That puts four in the correct spot. And the only thing left to do is return that index, that swap index that is pointing to the four. And this is everything that the pivot function is going to do. And the reason we return the index is when we run quicksort again on this side.

We're going to do it from the beginning up to but not including the swap index. And then on the other side it's going to be the swap index plus one to the end. So that is why we need to return the index. So we'll start coding this in the next video. But for now that is our overview of pivot.

LECTURE 21.5: Quick Sort: Big O
----------------------------------------

Now let's look at quicksort. Big O. Let's bring up our list here. So the first step with quicksort is to run pivot. When we run pivot our list ends up looking like this. And if you'll recall in the pivot function we had a for loop that went all the way through the list. Looking at each item, that process of running pivot is going to be o of n. But the recursive part. We've done one step here. Here's two steps and three steps. So let's say we have approximately eight items here.

And we did this in three steps. Two to the third power is eight. We've seen this before. That's O of log n. So quicksort is O of n times log n, just like merge sort. And on the graph that is much more efficient. Then O of n squared. But the n times log n for quicksort is actually just for the best case and the average case. The worst case is not n times log n. So let's take a look at that. Our worst case is if we already have sorted data. So we start out with our pivot point being that one, we're not going to move anything else in the graph, and that one is going to be sorted.

And normally we run quicksort on everything on the left and everything on the right. But we only have items on the right. So we're going to run pivot on everything else. Again. Remember pivot is O of n. So we're going to do this again. And then we're going to do it again all the way down. So we're running that pivot function on every single item. So that O of n function pivot, we're running it in times in order to get this sorted. In other words this is O of n squared if you have already sorted data.

So O of n squared is actually the worst case with quicksort. So long as you don't have sorted data, you know you're going to have data that is very out of order. You can count on it running an n times log n times, but if you have sorted data, it might be better to use something like insertion sort. So we saw earlier that insertion sort is generally O of n squared. But the best possible scenario for insertion sort is O of n. So that becomes one of the things you have to consider when you're looking at sorting algorithm.

Big O.

SECTION 22: Dynamic Programming
########################################

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 22.1: Overlapping Subproblems
----------------------------------------

Welcome to Dynamic programming. Now there are two things that are required for something to be able to use dynamic programming. We'll talk about one of those things in this video and one of those things in the next video. So the first requirement is going to be overlapping subproblems. And that might sound like a complicated turn, but this is not a complicated concept. We have already seen in this course where sometimes you can take a problem, break it down into multiple subproblems, solve each of the subproblems, put them all back together, and solve an overall problem.

With overlapping subproblems. It looks a little bit different with overlapping subproblems. You're going to have some problems that repeat. So for example subproblem number one all of these subproblems are identical. Same is true of subproblem number two and subproblem number three. So when we say overlapping subproblems we are really saying repeating subproblems. So let's say for example, we have that first subproblem subproblem number one. And let's just say it's incredibly processor intensive and it's a trillion operations.

And when we get all done running this it returns the number ten. Well when we move to the next subproblem that is identical, we already know it should return the number ten. So it would be really inefficient to do those trillion operations again. So one of the things that we could do is take that number ten and store it in an array list. And since that was subproblem number one, we'll take that ten and put it at the index of one. So when we move to this next subproblem, we could check to see if we have a value at the index of one.

And because we do this becomes an O of one operation. So now we'll move to that next subproblem. Let's say that one's a trillion operations as well. And it's going to return the number 20. Of course we're going to store that at the index of two. And the same thing for subproblem number three. And we'll store that at the index of three. Now when we move to these four subproblems we can pull up each one of these answers like this as an O of one operation, which of course is much more efficient.

And now that we have the answers to all of these subproblems, we can put them all back together and solve the overall problem. So this process of storing the answers to these subproblems is called memoization. Now it's not memorization. It is memoization. So now that we've looked at this concept of overlapping subproblems, I'm going to bring something back up that we looked at earlier in the course that uses subproblems. But they're not overlapping subproblems.

And that is merge sort. So when merge sort, the subproblem of breaking this in half is different than breaking either of these two in half. And all of these are different than breaking these in half. These are all unique problems. None of these overlap. And that is to say, none of these subproblems are repeating subproblems. They're all different and unique. Likewise putting these first two items together, this five and this four, and putting them together where they're sorted.

Is a particular subproblem that is different than putting together a seven and a one. And both of these are different than putting together a three and a two, etc.. And putting these together our unique subproblems and so on. So I want to emphasize that merge sort is not a candidate for dynamic programming. It has subproblems, but it does not have overlapping subproblems. And that is the first thing that is required to be able to use dynamic programming.

In the next video, we're going to talk about how you also need to have an optimized substructure. But for now, that is our overview of overlapping subproblems.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 22.2: Optimized Substructure
----------------------------------------

So in this video we're going to talk about the second requirement for dynamic programming. And that is an optimized substructure. Now optimized substructure sounds really complicated but it is a pretty simple concept. It's an extension of what we saw in the last video. In the last video, we took a problem and we broke it down into subproblems. And they needed to be overlapping subproblems. But there's something else about these subproblems that has to be true.

They have to have an optimized substructure. So to explain what that is I'm going to bring up a weighted graph. Let's say we want to go from A to D and we want to have the lowest cost path. Well there are two ways to get there. We can go a C, D and the total cost would be 50. The 30 plus the 20. Or we can go this way and the cost is 25. The ten plus the 15. Obviously this would be the better path. But in order to get from A to D we have a couple of subproblems.

We have the sub problem of how do we go from A to B, and the sub problem of how do we go from B to D. And what makes this an optimized substructure is if you get the optimal way of going from A to B, and the optimal way of going from B to D, it gives you the optimal way of going from A to D. So going back and looking at the subproblems like this, you have to have an optimized substructure, which means if you have the optimal solution for subproblem one and subproblem two and subproblem three, that's going to give you the optimal solution to the overall problem.

Now, to show something that does not have an optimal substructure, I'm going to bring back that same weighted graph. But now instead of doing the lowest cost path, we're going to do the highest cost path, which is going to be this. Now I want to point out this is the highest cost path that doesn't have any overlap or retracing, because you could just go from A to C, back to A, back to C infinitely and have an infinite cost path. So this is doing it with no overlapping.

So now let's look at what is the highest cost path from A to C. It is not to go directly from A to C, it is to go all the way around like this. And the highest cost path to go from C to D is to go all the way around like this. So in this situation, you can't take the optimal solution to each subproblem and solve the overall problem. So this is our second requirement for dynamic programming. Remember, the first one is that you have to have overlapping subproblems.

And the second one is that you need to have an optimized substructure.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 22.3: Fibonacci Sequence
----------------------------------------

Now we're going to create a Fibonacci sequence. And this is a very common way of teaching dynamic programming. In a Fibonacci sequence. We take those two numbers, add them together, and that gives us the next number in the sequence. Then we take the last two numbers and add those together, and we get the next number in the sequence. So then we take two and three, add those together and get five, and then we have eight and 13 and so on. And this sequence will grow very fast.

Sometimes you'll see a Fibonacci sequence the way we have it on the screen, and sometimes you'll see it beginning with a zero. And this still works because zero plus one does equal the next number in the sequence, which is one. It is more common to see it like this in mathematics. And the reason for that is if you think about 100 years ago before computers, when you were doing a Fibonacci sequence. You would say at the position of one is the value of one at the position of two is the value of one, position of three is the value of two, and so on.

But in computer science, an array starts with an index of zero, so it makes sense to put the zero in as part of the sequence. So I'm going to shrink this down because we need a little bit more room on the screen. And I'm going to remove these numbers and bring up a call stack. And then we're going to start our code out like this. And I'm going to call our method fib because Fibonacci is kind of long. And then we're going to pass this an integer in which represents the position in the Fibonacci sequence.

So in this case we're going to find fib of two which is going to add an instance to the call stack with n equal to two. And remember we're going to add the values that are at the previous two positions. So we'll do that with this line of code. We're going to return fib of n minus one plus fib of n minus two. Now I want to point out that picture of the array on the bottom is actually not going to exist in this code. We're not going to create the array.

I'm just keeping it there because it makes it easier to keep track of what we're doing. So it's just a visual to help keep track. So the first thing we're going to do here is run fib of n minus one. And that's a recursive call of the fib method. So that adds an instance onto the call stack with the value of one. And this brings us to our base case. So we'll say if n is equal to zero or n is equal to one. So both zero and one are base cases. We're going to return in.

So in this case n was one. So we're going to return one. Now remember that's not actually going into an array. There's no array in the code here. I'm just doing this so it's easier to keep track of. But because we had that return statement this gets popped from the call stack. Now this instance with the value of two is the active instance. We already calculated fib of n minus one. Now we move to fib of n minus two. And that adds an instance with the value of zero.

And that's going to trigger this conditional in the if statement where n is equal to zero. And then we return n which is zero. And because that's a return statement this gets popped from the call stack. Now those two instances that have been popped from the call stack have returned the result of fib of n minus one and fib of n minus two. So now we can return the value of one. And then this gets popped from the call stack. Now I wanted to walk through this with the call stack because that's how we have done this previously in the course.

But I'm going to drop this call, stack out and represent these three method calls like this. We called fib of two and then it called fib of one and then fib of zero three different method calls. Now let's look at what we would have to do to do fib of three. FIB of three is going to be fib of two plus fib of one. And when you get the solution to fib of two plus fib of one, that's going to give you two. Now, if you take a look at this pyramid that we have here of method calls, you can see that we have a duplicate which is fib of one.

That means that we have overlapping subproblems. And that meets one of the requirements for dynamic programming. And then the other requirement is an optimized substructure. So if you have the optimized solution for each one of these subproblems, it gives you the optimized solution for the overall problem, which means that this has an optimized substructure, which is the other requirement for dynamic programming. So let's move this to the middle of the screen and build this out further.

FIB of four would look like this. And you can already start to see that this is going to grow very quickly. So what I'm going to do to make it where we can fit everything on the screen is every time you see fib of two. Obviously there's fib of one and fib of zero under each of these. In order to make more room, I'm going to remove these from the diagram. So this is not to say that these methods aren't running. They are. I'm just going to take them out of the diagram so we have more room.

So then I'll tighten this up a little bit like this. And then we can look at fib of five. And then this is what we have to do for fib of six. And this is what we have to do for fib of seven. So this tree has a lot of redundancy in it, which means we can make this a lot more efficient, which we will do in the next video. But for now I want to point out, for example, fib of five. Each of these have the same pyramid of method calls under them. And then if you look at fib of four, each of these have the same pyramid of method calls under them.

So that gives us a lot of room to make this more efficient. And that's going to be important because just getting to fib of seven, there are 25 method calls on the screen. But remember every time we had a fib of two, there were two more method calls underneath each of these. So this is 16 more method calls or a total of 41 just to calculate fib of seven. So I'm going to drop these out. And then I'm going to bring up this chart from big O Cheat Sheet.

The big O of doing it this way is O of two to the n. And that is far less efficient than O of n squared, which means it's really bad from a big O perspective. LECTURE 22.4: Memoization
----------------------------------------

In this video we're going to talk about memoization. And we're going to start out by bringing up the code from the last video. And writing it this way was very inefficient. So we're going to change this code to use memoization. So the first thing we're going to do is create an array called memo. So the reason why I'm using a capital I integer instead of an int. Is that it will initialize our array like this. And having null in each one of those indexes is going to make our code a little bit easier to write.

So now let's assume that these are already populated, that these have already been memoized. If this is the case, we can make this much more efficient by just going directly to that index and returning the value. So we'll do that like this. We'll say if memo at the index of n is not equal to null. And let's say we're looking at the index of seven, it is indeed not null. We'll just return memo at the index of n. And that is an O of one operation.

But now let's look at how our code works. If we don't have a value at that index. Neither of the if statements apply. The first thing that we would do is look at that first if statement. And that only applies if memo at the index of n is not equal to null, and in this case it is null. So then we go to the next if statement. That only applies if n is equal to 0 or 1. And that brings us down to this line. And this returns the value of fib of n minus one plus fib of n minus two.

But the problem with this is it doesn't store that value in the array. So we want to remove this return statement. And instead we want to take that value and add it to the array like this. And that will add that 13 to the array. So we'll return it like this. We'll just say return memo at the index of n and that returns that 13. So one of the things I want to point out here is technically the first two indexes are never going to have a number. The first two indexes will always look like this.

And the reason for that is if you look at this if statement. This will always return in and because it has a return statement in it. We are never going to get down to this line. And this is the line that actually adds values to the array. But I am going to put these values here because effectively, if the index is 0 or 1, you will have the 0 or 1 returned. And it just makes it easier to keep track of what we're doing if I put those in there. But I want to point that out, in case you print out that memo array, that you won't be surprised that those are both null.

Now I'm going to take all of these indexes and change them to null and walk through how we would do this with memoization. So I will start out by bringing up a call stack. And if we were going to find fib of seven, we would start out by adding an instance with seven to the call stack. So now let's walk through the code. That first if statement does not apply because at the index of seven it is null. And the second if statement doesn't apply. So that brings us down to this line.

And the first thing we do in that line is call fib of n minus one, which in this case is going to be fib of six. Now fib of six is the active instance on the call stack. And once again neither of the if statements apply. So the first thing that fib of six is going to do is call fib of n minus one, which is going to be fib of five. Feb oh five is going to call fib of four, fib of four is going to call fib of three, and so on. So I wanted to illustrate that.

So when we go to this diagram this will make more sense. We start out by putting fib of seven on the call stack. And it calls fib of six and it calls fib of five, and so on until we get down to fib of one which is our base case. So if we go back and look at our code, it is this if statement that applies. And this applies if n equals zero or if n equals one. In either of those circumstances, we're going to return in which in this case is one, and we're going to look at n being equal to zero here in a moment.

So let's go ahead and bring this back because we just ran a return statement. FIB of one will now be popped from the call stack. And instead of removing that I'm going to color it in purple so we can keep track of how many method calls we have. So now fib of two is the active instance on the call stack again. And it's going to call fib of zero. And we're going to use that same if statement and return zero. When we do that fib of zero is popped from the call stack and we color that in purple.

So now fib of two is the active instance again. So now let's go back and look at our code. It is these two lines that apply. Now both of these instances fib of n minus one and fib of n minus two have returned their values. So now we can store that in memo of index of n, which in this case is the index of two. So that stores that value at the index of two. And then the next step we return that value of one to the calling method. But the important thing here is this is the first time that we have stored a value in this array, which is to say this is the first time we have memoized something.

Because remember at the index of 011 those values aren't actually stored in there. This is the first one we've actually stored. And that's going to make everything go much faster. So let's bring back our diagram here. FIB of two will now be popped from the call stack. We'll color that in purple. Now fib of three is the active instance on the call stack. FIB of three is going to call fib of one. And remember if we have fib of zero or fib of one, that is our base case that's going to return one.

And that gets popped from the call stack. Now fib of three can add one plus one and get two and store that in the array and return that value which will pop fib of three from the call stack. So now fib of four is the active instance on the call stack. It is going to call fib of two. And this is the first time that we're calling a method where this value has been memoized. Remember, fib of two will normally call fib of one and fib of zero. But now this if statement applies.

There is a value at that index. So we can just return that value. And that means we never get down to this line that would call fib of n minus one and fib of n minus two, and those would be fib of one and fib of zero. So we can just drop these out of the diagram. Those never get run. So now we can take that three and add it to the array. So now fib of four is done running and it will be popped from the call stack. Now fib of five is the active instance on the call stack.

It calls fib of three. But because fib of three has been memoized, we don't need to run these two instances. I'll drop these out of the diagram, and this will return the value at index of three as an O of one operation and be popped from the call stack. So now we can add the value for fib of five to the array. And now fib of five will be popped from the call stack. And we move to fib of six which we'll call fib of four. And that has been memoized.

Which means we don't need to run any of these methods. I'll drop these out. We can just return the value at the index of four. And then this is popped from the call stack. So now fib of six can add this to the array. And once it does that and returns it it is popped from the call stack. Now fib of seven is going to call fib of five. This has been memoized which means we don't need to run any of these methods. I can drop these out. FIB of five will return the value at the index of five and then be popped from the call stack.

Now fib of seven can add fib of six and fib of five. Add 13 to the array and it will return that and it will be popped from the call stack. So if you look at this diagram we have 13 method calls. And this is already a lot better than what we saw in the last video where we had 41 method calls to solve fib of seven. So the big O for this is O of two n minus one. In in this case was seven. Two times seven is 14, minus one is 13. Method calls. So of course we're going to simplify this.

The first thing that we'll do is drop the non-dominant. And now we have O of two n. And I'm going to leave that two here for a moment to illustrate something. If you were going to do fib of eight, how many additional method calls would you need to have. And the answer is two. And then if you did fib of nine, there would be two more every time you go up a value it is two additional method calls. But of course we're going to do this simplification where we drop the constant and this becomes o of n.

So by using memoization, we have gone all the way from O of two to the N, which is incredibly inefficient all the way to O of n. So let's bring our code back up. And just like I did in the last video, I'm going to create a variable called counter. And then we'll increment counter every time we have a new method call. So now let's flip over to IntelliJ and take a look at this. So there is the code that we just wrote. And then I'm going to scroll up here.

And this is identical to what we did in the last video. Where we start out with n being equal to seven. So I'll run this and you can see that fib of seven is 13 and the counter is also 13. So I'm going to come up here and change n to be equal to 40. And remember in the last video when we did n equal to 40 there were over 300 million method calls. So I'll run this and you can see that fib of 40 is that very large number there. But the counter is only 79.

So remember that's two n minus one. So two times 40 is 80 minus one is 79. So this gives us an incredible increase in efficiency. And that is the power of memoization.

----------------------------------------
LECTURE 22.5: Bottom Up
----------------------------------------

When you're doing dynamic programming, your solution can either be top down or bottom up. When we did this recursively, it was top down. That is to say, the first thing that we looked at was the highest number. Then we moved down until we looked at fib of zero. But when we do it iteratively, we are doing this bottom up. We look first at the smallest numbers and then we start working our way up. So let's put this in an array. And once again I'm going to bring back our recursive solution.

When we did this recursively we started by looking at the index of seven. We started on the right side of the array. Now when we do this iteratively we're going to start on the left side of the array. Or you could say the bottom side and work up. So we'll start our iterative code out like this. So the first thing that we'll do is create an array. And the size of the array will be n plus one. So if we want to find fib of seven we're going to need an array that has eight spots because it is zero through seven.

And that is why it is n plus one. And we're going to create a new array every time we run this. The next thing we'll do is populate those first two spots in the array with a zero and a one. And then we're going to have a for loop. Notice that index starts at two. And the reason for that is the first open index that we need to fill is the index of two. Also notice this is less than or equal to n because we have to go up to and including the index of seven.

So now we have to add the values at index of zero and one. And put that at the index of two. Will do that with this line of code. So we're going to say fib list at index. And for this iteration of the for loop that's going to be two. At the index of two we're going to set that to be equal to the values at index minus one and index minus two, which is going to be one. Did we iterate through the for loop again? Now index is three and when we add the values at index minus one and index minus two, that is going to be two.

Then we keep iterating through the for loop until we get to the index of seven. And that gives us 13. So now we want to return that value of 13. We'll do it like this. We'll say return five less at the index of n, which in this case is the index of seven which returns that 13. So the big O for this is O of n minus one. So the index was seven. And we iterated through the for loop six times. But of course we'll simplify this and drop the non-dominant.

And this becomes o of n. So now let's take this code and we'll do the same thing we did in the last two videos and add a counter. And will increment this inside of the for loop. So now let's flip over to IntelliJ and take a look at this. So there is the code that we just wrote. And then I'm going to scroll up. And I'm doing the same thing here that I did in the last two videos, where I start out with n being equal to seven. And I'll run this. So fib of seven is 13 and the counter is six.

So now I'm going to come up here and change this to 40. Remember when we did this recursively without memoization there were over 300 million method calls. And when we do this bottom up, we have 39 method calls. So you're able to do this very efficiently by either doing this recursively with memoization or doing this iteratively. Now there's one more thing I want to point out about doing this iteratively bottom up. So I'm going to flip back when we look at the code the way that we have written it here, we're going to iterate through the for loop until we get to the index of n.

And then we return the value at the index of n. And as we discussed this is going to be o of n. But what if we want to find fib of seven a second time. Well the next time we run this these three lines will be the first thing that we run, which creates a brand new array that looks like this. Then we have to iterate through the for loop and populate that array. So the second time that we run this is also going to be o of n. So the question you might have is can we use memoization and preserve these answers just like we did with the recursive solution.

And the answer is yes. If you used memoization the second time you ran this and you ran fib of seven, the second time would be O of one, because we could just pull that value out of the array. But generally with an iterative bottom up solution, we don't do it that way. There is a downside to using memoization. And that is you have this array that is in memory. You're using up memory space. To be able to get a better time complexity. And when you do this iteratively, you have a pretty good time complexity of O of N.

So to simplify this and to not use the extra memory space, generally we don't use memoization in each additional time you call. This will also be o of n. And that's why we're going to leave our iterative solution just like this. And when we do dynamic programming iteratively, remember that is also known as bottom up.
